after the threats, the argument and the refusal, the prophet does not answer the notables with a threat
of his own but with the declaration that upon God he relies and the prayer that God judge — so that the
last word of the confrontation is not expulsion but entrusting. **[Qurṭubī]**'s report of the theological
reading of "unless God wills" makes the same movement in doctrine: the man who has been saved from error
does not guarantee his own future but places it, with everything else, in the knowledge and will of the
One who encompasses all things.

## 7:90

*"And the notables of his people who disbelieved said, 'If you follow Shuʿayb, indeed you will then be the losers.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse: the assembly of the disbelieving men of Shuʿayb's people — the
notables who denied God's signs and denied His messenger and persisted in their error — said to others of
their people: if you follow Shuʿayb in what he says and answer him in what he calls you to of God's
oneness and submission to His command and prohibition, and affirm his prophethood, then you are surely
losers: cheated in what you have done and in your leaving the religion you are established on for the
religion he calls you to, and destroyed by that act of yours [Ṭabarī]. **[Study Quran]** explains the
warning's content: the notables, or leaders among the disbelievers, warned that those who followed
Shuʿayb would be the losers — meaning those who are deceived with regard to their religion, and that
abandoning their native religious practice to follow Shuʿayb would lead to destruction (Ṭ); in v. 92,
however, it is those who denied Shuʿayb who were the losers [Study Quran]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the
verse in the narrative: God describes the enormity of the disbelief, rebellion, transgression and
misguidance of Shuʿayb's people and the defiance of truth rooted in their hearts — they vowed, saying,
"if you follow Shuʿayb, be sure then you will be the losers"; and God answered them: "so the earthquake
seized them and they lay prostrate in their homes — those who belied Shuʿayb became as if they had never
dwelt there; those who belied Shuʿayb, they were the losers" (7:91–92) [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Maʿārif]** notes
the setting of the statement: the arrogant chiefs of the people said it while talking among themselves, or
said it to their followers — that is, if you are to follow Shuʿayb, then you are sure losers
(al-Baḥr al-Muḥīṭ from ʿAṭāʾ) [Maʿārif]. **[Jalālayn]** reads it briefly: the council of those of his
people who disbelieved — that is, some said to others — "verily if you follow Shuʿayb you shall indeed be
losers," the *lām* being for an oath [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** draws the reversal the sūrah will soon state:
the notables call following the prophet a loss, and the verses that follow show who in fact lost
[Saʿdī].

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** notes the word *al-khusrān*: the losers are the cheated and the destroyed —
the word carries the sense of a trade gone wrong and of a life lost, so that the notables' warning
describes their own end in the vocabulary they meant for their followers [Ṭabarī]. **[Jalālayn]** notes
the oath embedded in "if you follow" — the *lām* of swearing [Jalālayn].

**Cross-references.** "Those who belied Shuʿayb, they were the losers" (7:92); "Indeed, the losers are
those who lose themselves and their families on the Day of Resurrection" (39:15); "Say: shall we inform
you of the greatest losers in their deeds?" (18:103); "And those who disbelieved say, 'If you follow the
way, you will be carried off from your land'" (cf. 28:57) [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** **[Study Quran]**'s observation that v. 92 will name the deniers themselves as the
losers gives the verse its place in the sūrah's pattern of reversal: in story after story the accusation
made by the powerful returns as the verdict on them — the people of Noah called the prophet astray, the
people of Hūd called him a fool, and here the notables of Midian call following the prophet a loss.
**[Maʿārif]**'s note that the sentence was spoken among themselves or to their followers makes the
warning a piece of politics rather than an argument: it is not addressed to Shuʿayb but to those who might
listen to him.
## 7:91

*"So the earthquake seized them, and morning found them lying lifeless in their homes."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse as the sequel to the taunt of 7:90: the quaking seized those of Shuʿayb's people who disbelieved, and they became, in their dwelling, *jāthimīn* — fallen on their knees, dead and destroyed. The word *al-rajfah* he had already explained earlier in the sūrah: it is the earthquake, the convulsion by which God's chastisement shakes a people, and he repeats that explanation here [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same primary sense — *al-rajfah* is *al-zalzalah*, the earthquake — and notes the alternative reported in the tafsīrs: it was *al-ṣayḥah*, the Cry. He adds the distinction that the people of al-Aykah were destroyed by *al-ẓullah*, the overshadowing cloud [Qurṭubī]. **[Ṭabarī]** carries the fullest narrative, from al-Suddī: God sent Shuʿayb to Madyan and to the companions of al-Aykah — and *al-aykah* is the thicket of trees — who, along with their unbelief, used to short-change in measure and weight. He called them and they denied him and demanded the punishment. When they grew insolent and denied him, God opened for them a door from the doors of Gehenna, and the heat from it destroyed them, so that neither shade nor water profited them; then He sent a cloud in which was a wholesome wind, and they found the wind's coolness and sweetness and called to one another, "The shade-canopy! Gather under it!" When their men, women and children had all gathered beneath the cloud, it folded over them and destroyed them — and that is His saying, "So the punishment of the Day of the Shadow seized them" (26:189) [Ṭabarī]. Ibn Isḥāq, cited in the same passage, reports that Shuʿayb's people were people of unfair dealing with people's measures and weights together with their disbelief in God and their denial of their prophet, and that Shuʿayb kept calling them to God, to His worship, and to leaving off wronging people — saying to them, as admonition: "And I do not desire to differ from you in that which I forbid you; I desire only reform so far as I am able, and my success is only through God; upon Him I rely and to Him I turn" (11:88) [Ṭabarī]. **[Study Quran]** draws out the parallel that governs the passage: the description of the destruction of the Midianites is identical to that of the Thamūd, the people of Ṣāliḥ (see 7:78); it is reported that when the people of Madyan denied Shuʿayb, God first sent upon them such a terrible heat that even shade and water could provide no relief, then sent a cloud bearing cool, refreshing breezes, and when all the people had gathered beneath it, it unleashed upon them flame and fire (Ibn Kathīr, Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī), followed by a terrible cry and an earthquake that instantly extinguished their spirits and left them suddenly lifeless (Ibn Kathīr) [Study Quran]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** frames the verse as the answer to the vow of the notables: they said, "If you follow Shuʿayb, you will then be the losers," and God answered them with the earthquake, the houses, and the verdict that follows in 7:92 [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** reads it briefly and exactly: *al-rajfah* is the violent earthquake, and "they became in their dwelling *jāthimīn*" means struck down, dead, motionless [Saʿdī]. **[Jalālayn]** renders the same in a line: the quaking seized them and they lay lifeless, keeled over on their knees, in their homes [Jalālayn].

**Language.** The root *r-j-f* carries shaking and commotion: *rajafa* is to tremble, *rajjafa* to cause to tremble, and the noun *rajfah* used here has been taken by the sūrah's commentators as the earthquake itself and, by others, as the Cry that accompanies it [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Jāthim* denotes a body settled on its knees, a corpse that has come down and stayed down; the root's sense of a creature fixed in place — as a bird *jāthim* on the ground — gives the image its force: a whole town of bodies brought down and made to stay [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** preserves the same lexical sense, and adds that the words *laylatan*/*yanāmūn* counterparts elsewhere in the sūrah gather the same field of meaning, the living caught in the places they thought were safe [Qurṭubī].

**Cross-references.** "But the earthquake seized them, and they became within their home [corpses] fallen prone" (7:78, of Thamūd) and "So the earthquake seized them and they lay lifeless in their homes" (29:37, of Shuʿayb's people); "But the punishment of the Day of the Shadow seized them" (26:189); "And when Our command came, We saved Shuʿayb and those who believed with him by a mercy from Us, and the Cry seized those who did wrong, and they became within their homes [corpses] fallen prone" (11:94); "So the punishment seized them in the morning" (cf. 15:83, of Thamūd) [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The sūrah has now closed its third and fourth prophetic cycles — Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb — with the same words: the earthquake, the homes, the bodies fallen. **[Study Quran]**'s observation that the Midianite destruction is "identical to that of the Thamūd" is more than a note on style; it is the religious argument of the whole section. The towns of the prophets end in one pattern because the crime repeated in them is one crime, and God's answer to it does not vary. And the narrative's detail from al-Suddī — men, women and children gathered by their own choice under the very cloud that kills them [Ṭabarī] — is the image of a people who run toward the shade when the sun burns, and do not ask what the shade holds.

## 7:92

*"Those who denied Shuʿayb — it was as though they had never dwelt there. Those who denied Shuʿayb, it is they who were the losers."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse as God's own narration of the ruin: He destroyed those who denied Shuʿayb and did not believe in him, and made their town empty and void — "as though they had never dwelt in it, never settled and never lived there" — and then, naming them a second time, He pronounces: those who denied Shuʿayb are the ones who were the losers [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** records the grammatical observation of al-Jurjānī that the sentence is a fresh one (*kalām mustaʾnaf*): those who denied Shuʿayb became as though they had never ceased to be dead; and the second half, "those who denied Shuʿayb — they were the losers," is likewise a fresh address (*ibtidāʾ al-khiṭāb*), a doubling of the blame and the rebuke and a repetition meant to magnify the matter; so that where they had said, "whoever follows Shuʿayb is a loser," God answered that the losers are precisely those who said it [Qurṭubī]. **[Saʿdī]** unfolds the picture the words compress: as though they had never stayed in their dwellings, never enjoyed their courtyards, never sat in their shade, never sung beside their running water, never eaten of the fruit of their trees — the punishment took them and moved them from the dwelling of amusement and play to the dwelling of grief, misery, chastisement and depths. And the loss is entirely theirs, because they lost themselves and their families on the Day of Resurrection — "that is the manifest loss" (39:15) — not those to whom they had said, "If you follow Shuʿayb you will surely be losers" [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** states the same reversal: the people of Madyan were so thoroughly effaced by the earthquake that it was as though they had never dwelt there; and in response to the disbelievers' description of Shuʿayb's followers as the losers (7:90), this verse affirms that it is the disbelievers who were the losers — deceived or deluded about true religion, and destroyed as a result [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** notes the softening of *ka-an* ("as if") and the function of the repeated relative clause: it refutes what they had said before [Jalālayn]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** places the two halves together with the verse before: the earthquake seized them, they lay prostrate in their homes, they became as if they had never dwelt there — those who belied Shuʿayb, they were the losers [Ibn Kathīr].

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** establishes the key verb: *yaghnaw fīhā*, from *ghaniya fulān bi-makān kadhā* — a person dwelt in such a place, and *al-maghnā* is the dwelling-place; he cites the poet ʿUbayd b. al-Abraṣ, "And your neighbours dwelt there once *[wa-laqad yaghnā bihā jīrānuki]*..." — poetry of a place from which the neighbours have gone [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same lexicon and adds the verse of Labīd and the line of Ḥātim al-Ṭāʾī, where the root covers dwelling and wealth both — *ghanīnā zamānan*: we dwelt, and we were sufficient, for a time; the town that was their dwelling was also, in the root's other sense, their self-sufficiency, and the verse erases both together [Qurṭubī]. The repetition of "those who denied Shuʿayb" is, in the rhetoricians' terms, a repetition for magnification, not redundancy [Qurṭubī] [Jalālayn].

**Cross-references.** "And as if they had never dwelt therein" (cf. 11:68, of Thamūd; 28:58, of destroyed towns); "So travel through the earth and see how was the end of those who denied" (7:84; cf. 3:137; 6:11); "Indeed, the losers are those who lose themselves and their families on the Day of Resurrection — that is the manifest loss" (39:15); "Say: shall we inform you of the greatest losers in their deeds?" (18:103); "You will surely be the losers" — their own verdict, returned upon them (7:90) [Saʿdī] [Study Quran] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Reflection.** The verse is built on a reversal the sūrah loves: the word the powerful used for the believers — *loss* — is taken from their mouths and returned to them. **[Qurṭubī]** captures the mechanism exactly: they said "whoever follows Shuʿayb is a loser," and God said the losers are those who said it [Qurṭubī]. And the first half of the verse supplies the proof of the second: "as though they had never dwelt there" is the archaeology of loss. Entire towns — houses, wells, orchards, children — were reduced to a sentence in a story. The verse teaches the reader the vocabulary of profit and loss in God's ledger, where the firmest-seeming holdings can be erased in a night, and the only wealth that survives the earthquake is the wealth of the world to come [Saʿdī].

## 7:93

*"So he turned away from them and said, 'O my people, I have conveyed to you the messages of my Lord and advised you sincerely; so how should I grieve over a disbelieving people?'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse in the aftermath of the destruction: Shuʿayb turned his back on them, departing from their midst when God's punishment came, and said — when he had become certain that God's vengeance had descended on the people who denied him, out of sorrow for them — "O my people, I conveyed to you the messages of my Lord": I delivered to you what He sent me with, the warning of His wrath for your persisting in disbelief in Him and in wronging people of their goods; "and I advised you sincerely": by commanding you to obey God and forbidding you His disobedience; "so how should I grieve over a disbelieving people" — how should I sorrow and ache for a people who denied God's oneness and denied His messenger? [Ṭabarī]. He records the early authorities on the verb: Ibn ʿAbbās, by ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭalḥah, and al-Suddī: *fakayfa āsā* means "how should I grieve?" And Ibn Isḥāq reports that grief for his people struck Shuʿayb as he saw God's vengeance upon them, and then he said this, consoling himself: "O my people, I conveyed to you the messages of my Lord and advised you — so how should I grieve over a disbelieving people?" [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]**, closing his commentary on the same passage, handles the sentence as a unit: *āsā* is from *al-asā*, sorrow; the interrogative at the end is rhetorical and means negation [Qurṭubī] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** hears a reproach in it: when they perished, their prophet turned away from them and said — censuring them and addressing them after their death — "O my people, I conveyed to you the messages of my Lord": I delivered them, made them clear, until they reached from you the utmost a message can reach and entered your hearts; "and I advised you": but you did not accept my counsel and did not submit to my guidance, but rather transgressed and grew insolent — "so how should I grieve over a disbelieving people," a people in whom there is no good, to whom good came and who rejected it, whom nothing suits but evil? Such people are not worthy of grief; rather their destruction is cause for relief. And he closes with a prayer of refuge: "Which misery and which punishment is more eloquent than a people reaching a state in which the sincerest of creation disavows them?" [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** sets the verse in the sūrah's pattern: Shuʿayb's address, except for its last sentence, is identical to Ṣāliḥ's address at 7:79; and the same question arises as there — was the address delivered after the people had been thoroughly destroyed, or only after the prophet became certain that destruction was imminent and unavoidable? (see 7:79 and commentary) [Study Quran].

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** gives the morphology: *asā* / *āsā*, sorrow; the noun *asan*, grief; *rajul āsin*, a grieving man — the interrogative *kayfa* with the imperfect gives the sense "how should I, of all people, grieve?" [Qurṭubī]. **[Ṭabarī]** glosses the two verbs with the vocabulary of the prophetic office itself: *al-balāgh*, the delivering of the message, and *al-naṣīḥah*, pure counsel offered without self-interest — the two duties that no prophet's mission is without, and the two that no people can claim were withheld from them [Ṭabarī]. **[Jalālayn]** marks the rhetorical question as a negation: I do not grieve [Jalālayn].

**Rulings.** The verse functions as a statement of the limits of the prophet's responsibility: conveyance and sincere counsel are the messenger's part; belief is the people's; the outcome is God's [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī]. The commentators note that this is the same formula by which Noah closed his account — "But if you turn away, then no payment have I asked of you" (cf. 7:62) — and Ṣāliḥ his: "I have advised you sincerely, but you do not like advisers" (7:79) [Study Quran]. The grief that Shuʿayb at first felt and then renounced is not forbidden feeling but redundant feeling: the man who has discharged his office does not answer for those who refuse [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī].

**Cross-references.** "And he turned away from them and said, 'O my people, I had certainly conveyed to you the messages of my Lord and advised you, so how should I grieve for a disbelieving people?'" — the near-identical address of Ṣāliḥ (7:79); "I have advised you sincerely, but you do not like advisers" (7:79); "And I do not want to differ from you in that which I forbid you; I only want reform so far as I am able" (11:88); "Not upon the Messenger is [anything] except clear notification" (5:99; cf. 24:54; 64:12); "And never would We destroy a town until We had sent to it warners" (26:208, cf. 26:209); "Perhaps you would kill yourself with grief, that they do not become believers" (18:6) [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Reflection.** The final question — *how should I grieve?* — is among the most complete statements in the Qurʾān of what the prophetic vocation is and is not. In the sūrah's telling, Shuʿayb had lived among these people, called them, warned them, endured their mockery (7:88–90), and then watched them end under the cloud. That he should feel sorrow was human — Ibn Isḥāq says the grief came first [Ṭabarī] — and the verse records what the messenger did with it: he named his own work truthfully, "I conveyed," "I advised," and then released the people to the judgment of God. There is a lesson here for every caller to God: the burden of results is not the burden of obedience, and the measure of faithfulness is the message delivered, not the number of those who receive it.

## 7:94

*"And We sent no prophet to any town but that We seized its people with hardship and adversity, so that they might humble themselves."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reports the purpose of the verse: God says this to His Prophet, informing him of His custom (*sunnah*) with the nations that passed away before his community, and reminding the disbelievers of Quraysh, so that they might hold back from what they were fixed upon of joining partners with God and denying His messenger. "We sent no prophet to a town" before you "but that We seized its people with *al-baʾsāʾ* and *al-ḍarrāʾ*" — and *al-baʾsāʾ* is misery, straitened living, narrowness; *al-ḍarrāʾ* is harm, and bad conditions in the affairs of their worldly life; "so that they might humble themselves" — We did this so that they would humble themselves before their Lord, grow still before Him, and turn back, by giving up their unbelief and repenting of denying their prophets. Al-Suddī glossed it as poverty and hunger [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** quotes and expands the verse precisely in the register of affliction: God mentions the *baʾsāʾ* and *ḍarrāʾ* that struck the earlier nations to whom He sent prophets; *baʾsāʾ*, he says, refers to the physical sicknesses and diseases they suffered, while *ḍarrāʾ* refers to poverty and humiliation (note that he assigns the terms the other way round from Ṭabarī). "So that they might humble themselves" means: supplicate, humble themselves and call upon God that He might remove the afflictions they suffered. This verse indicates that God sent down severe afflictions so that they might invoke Him — but they did not do what He ordered; therefore He changed the affliction into prosperity to test them [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** gives the theology of the verse in one movement: God says He sent no prophet into a town calling them to the worship of God and forbidding them the evil they were in — and when they did not comply, He tested them "with *al-baʾsāʾ* and *al-ḍarrāʾ*," that is, with poverty, sickness and kinds of calamities, "so that their souls, when these struck them, might be humbled, so that they would entreat God and submit to the truth" [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** sets the verse in the structure of the sūrah: 7:94–102 begins a section that serves as a commentary on and conclusion to the prophetic narratives of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot and Shuʿayb; in both general and specific ways the Qurʾān indicates that the sending of a prophetic messenger is often accompanied by some form of adversity for his people as a moral test; the misfortune of this verse means poverty, hunger, illness and various forms of loss (see 6:42–44) [Study Quran]. **[Maʿārif]** supplies the hermeneutical frame and the moral: after relating the stories of the earlier people, these verses speak of warnings and lessons for the present people, to save them from the fate of their forefathers. The Qurʾān's historical style is deliberately selective: it does not describe an event in its entirety or in chronological order, but selects the relevant portion and lays emphasis on the lesson contained therein. Verse 94 warns that the fate of disaster and suffering described was not limited to the peoples of Nūḥ, ʿĀd and Thamūd — it is a usual practice of God that those who reject His prophets are subjected to suffering and distress so that they may turn to their Lord in repentance; it is human nature to turn to the Creator in distress; the suffering is, in fact, a blessing of God in disguise. He supports this with the verse of Rūmī: *The people are made to misbehave with you / in order that you turn to your Lord in tears* [Maʿārif]. **[Jalālayn]** compresses the same: God did not send a prophet to any city but that, when they denied him, He seized its people with misery — abject poverty — and hardship — illness — so that they might be humble and so believe [Jalālayn].

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** sets out the two terms: *al-baʾsāʾ* from *al-buʾs*, distress and hardship of living; *al-ḍarrāʾ* from *al-ḍurr*, harm — the pair became a fixed Qurʾānic couplet for the two hands of adversity, privation and pain [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Maʿārif]** record that the exegetes distribute the two differently — one group making *baʾsāʾ* poverty and *ḍarrāʾ* illness (Ibn Masʿūd is quoted to this effect), another reversing them — while agreeing on the whole [Ibn Kathīr] [Maʿārif]. **[Ṭabarī]** also notes a grammatical point of the word *yaḍḍarraʿūn*: the form is originally *yataḍarraʿūn*, "they humble themselves," the *tāʾ* having been assimilated into the *ḍād* because of the nearness of their points of articulation [Ṭabarī] [Jalālayn].

**Rulings.** The verse states a divine practice rather than a mechanical law: the sending of messengers is accompanied in the historical record by testing, and testing has a purpose stated in the verse itself — *laʿallahum yaḍḍarraʿūn*, "that haply they may humble themselves" [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī]. It is not permitted, however, to read the verse as a promise that every afflicted people is being reproved: **[Qurṭubī]**, in the following verse's commentary, insists that the verse's promise of opening applies to specific communities, "for the believers may be tried with straitened living, and it may be an expiation of their sins" [Qurṭubī].

**Cross-references.** "And We have already sent [messengers] to nations before you, [O Muhammad]; then We seized them with poverty and hardship that perhaps they might humble themselves" (6:42; cf. 6:43); "And We certainly seized the people of Pharaoh with years [of famine] and a shortage of fruits" (7:130); "Corruption has appeared throughout the land and sea by [reason of] what the hands of people have earned, so He may let them taste part of [the consequence of] what they have done, that perhaps they will return" (30:41); "So We seized them with hardship and adversity, that haply they might return" (cf. 23:76; 43:48) [Study Quran] [Ṭabarī].

**Reflection.** The verse answers a question the sūrah's stories have left echoing: why does God let a prophet's people suffer before they are destroyed? The answer is in the last word — *yaḍḍarraʿūn*, a verb of self-lowering, of turning toward. Hardship in the Qurʾān's view is not God's first language of punishment; it is His bedside manner, the touch that wakes the sleeping body before the last night comes. That is why **[Maʿārif]** can call the affliction "a blessing of God in disguise," and why **[Ibn Kathīr]** can pair the verse with the ḥadīth he cites in the next: the believer's whole affair is good, because hardship produces patience and ease produces gratitude [Ibn Kathīr]. The verse's tragedy is the response it reports elsewhere: "then when they forgot what they had been reminded of, We opened for them the gates of every thing" (6:44) — the door that should have led to humility became the door to ruin.

## 7:95

*"Then We replaced the evil with good, until they multiplied and said, 'Our fathers were touched by hardship and ease.' So We seized them suddenly, while they were unaware."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** proceeds step by step: "Then We replaced" for the people of that town "the evil" — and that evil was the *baʾsāʾ* and *ḍarrāʾ*, named "evil" only because it grievously affects people, while "the good" is ease, blessing and breadth of living. "Until they multiplied" — *ʿafaw* — until they grew numerous; and everything that becomes numerous in Arabic is said to have *ʿafā*, as in Labīd's verse about fattened camels. On *ʿafaw* he gathers the early authorities: Qatāda, "in place of severity, ease"; Mujāhid, "the evil is the evil, the good is ease, wealth and children"; Ibn ʿAbbās, "in place of severity, ease ... until they multiplied and their wealth multiplied"; al-Suddī, "until they multiplied"; the same from Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī; Ibn Zayd, "We replaced what they hated with what they loved in this world, until they multiplied from that punishment" — and he glossed their multiplication as like that of plants and feathers: they multiplied like vegetation and like plumage, then He took them suddenly while they were unaware. Only one early voice, Qatāda, took *ʿafaw* to mean "they rejoiced," and **[Ṭabarī]** rejects it: *al-ʿafw* in the sense of joy is not known in the speech of the Arabs; though it would be little different, he concedes, to say they rejoiced in their numbers and their wealth [Ṭabarī]. Then the verse's hard sentence: "and they said, Our fathers were touched by hardship and ease" — God's report that they interpreted the alternation itself as the standing custom of time, the way of the world, and nothing meant for them [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** continues the same in the moral key: God tested them with this and that so that they might humble themselves and repent, but they failed both tests — neither adversity nor prosperity made them change their ways — and they said, "We suffered hardship, then prosperity came as it had come to our fathers before us; it is a cycle in which we sometimes suffer and sometimes enjoy bounty." They did not comprehend God's wisdom, nor that He was testing them in both; the believers, by contrast, are grateful in good times and patient in hard times. He then cites the ḥadīth in the Ṣaḥīḥ: *Wonderful is the affair of the believer — nothing that God decrees for him but it is good for him: if harm strikes him he is patient, and that is good for him; and if ease comes to him he gives thanks, and that is good for him.* And the final clause: "So We seized them suddenly while they were unaware" — He struck them with the punishment all at once, and in this connection he cites the report: *Sudden death is a mercy for the believer and a remorseful seizure for the disbeliever* [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** notes the psychology of the interval in the same sequence: when the affliction did not reform them and their arrogance continued and their tyranny increased, God replaced hardship with good — pouring out provisions, curing their bodies, lifting the afflictions — "until they multiplied," that is, they grew numerous and their provisions multiplied and they expanded in God's bounty and forgot what had passed over them; and they said, "Hardship and ease have touched our fathers": this is a standing habit that has never ceased among the first and the last — sometime in ease, sometimes in hardship, sometimes joy, sometimes grief, according to the turning of time and the succession of days — and they supposed these things were not for admonition or remembrance, nor for drawing them on and rebuke. So when they exulted and rejoiced in what they were given, and the world was dearest to them, We seized them with the punishment "suddenly, while they did not perceive it" — no thought of ruin occurred to them, and they supposed they had power over what God had given them and would never be removed or transferred from it [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** reads the alternation as the test's design: "the good fortune that replaced their earlier adversity continued till they multiplied — that is, until they increased in both wealth and children (Ṭabarī), the two worldly goods that give people a false sense of security (cf. 7:97–99) with regard to both the vicissitudes of this life and punishment in the next"; it points to the parallel of 34:34–35, "And We sent no warner unto a town, but that those living in luxury there said: We disbelieve in that wherewith you have been sent" [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the sense of their speech in a line: it is nothing but the habit of time, not the consequence of God — so they remained as they were [Jalālayn].

**Language.** Four words carry the verse. *Al-sayyiʾah* and *al-ḥasanah* here are not moral qualities but states of circumstance: what grieves and what gladdens, hardship and ease [Ṭabarī]. *ʿAfaw* is to multiply and swell — the verb of increase, used both of hair and of wealth; Ṭabarī's poetry citation ties it to fattened camels, and Ibn Zayd to plants and plumage [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. *Baghtatan* is the adverbial "suddenly," the taking unawares; and "they do not perceive" repeats the note of the sūrah's questions at 7:97–98 [Ṭabarī]. And the pair *al-ḍarrāʾ* and *al-sarrāʾ* — harm and ease — is the fixed Qurʾānic couplet; the people's fatal sentence is their claim that the couplet explains itself [Saʿdī] [Study Quran].

**Belief.** The verse is a station in the sūrah's teaching about *istidrāj*: God's drawing a people on by degrees, opening the doors of good upon them when they persist, so that the giving itself becomes the punishment [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. It is also a study in gratitude and patience: the believer reads prosperity and adversity alike as address — this is the point of the ḥadīth of the Ṣaḥīḥ that **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites here, that neither state occurs to the believer except as good, because the two states call out the two virtues [Ibn Kathīr]. And it warns against the naturalist reading of one's own life: the interpretation the doomed people offered — "this is just how time goes" — is exactly the interpretation that makes God invisible in His own world [Saʿdī].

**Cross-references.** "So when they forgot that by which they had been reminded, We opened to them the doors of every [good] thing until, when they rejoiced in that which they were given, We seized them suddenly, and they were [then] in despair" (6:44; cf. 6:45); "And when adversity touches the human being, he calls on Us, and then when We bestow a favor upon him, he says, 'I was only given it because of knowledge'" (cf. 39:49; 41:50); "As for man, when his Lord tries him and is generous to him and favors him, he says, 'My Lord has honored me'" (89:15–16); "And how many a town did I grant enjoyment, then seized with the punishment" (cf. 22:45–48); "Have you not considered that We have given them enjoyment for years and then there came to them what they had been warned of?" (26:205–207) [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran] [Saʿdī].

**Reflection.** This is the psalm of the deceived, and every generation sings it. The people of Shuʿayb watched the pattern — hardship, then relief; ease, then loss — and concluded that the pattern explained itself; they called it the way of the world, and let the world go by. The verse replies that no sequence is self-explanatory: prosperity after affliction can be mercy answered by forgetfulness, and the very relief that seems like forgiveness may be the net drawn slowly. **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s two ḥadīths turn the whole verse into a mirror: the believer meets hardship with patience and ease with thanks, the disbeliever meets hardship with complaint and ease with heedlessness; and the "sudden" seizure comes upon the second, when he least expects that his security was borrowed. The reader of the sūrah has just been told twice, in 7:94 and here, what time is for — not a cycle, but a call.
## 7:96

*"And if the people of the towns had believed and been reverent, We would have opened upon them blessings from the heaven and the earth; but they denied, so We seized them for what they used to earn."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** — and here a note on the text belongs first: the great *Jāmiʿ al-Bayān* as it has come down to us carries no commentary on this verse or the two that follow it. The editor of the printed edition, Maḥmūd Shākir, records that the commentary on 7:96–98 is missing from the manuscripts, and that no one — not Ibn Kathīr, not al-Suyūṭī in *al-Durr al-Manthūr*, not al-Qurṭubī, not Abū Ḥayyān, not any of those expected to transmit from Abū Jaʿfar — ever quotes him on these three verses, which shows the gap to be ancient; he printed the verses alone and left the space blank *(note on the text)* [Ṭabarī]. The other sources fill the passage. **[Qurṭubī]** begins with the word: *qarya* is said of a town because people gather in it, from *qaraytu al-māʾ* — I gathered the water; then he reads the verse: "if the people of the towns had believed," that is, affirmed; "and been reverent," that is, of *shirk*; "We would have opened upon them blessings from the heaven and the earth," meaning rain and vegetation. And he adds the qualification that keeps the verse from becoming a slogan: this was said of specific communities, "for believers may be tried with straitened living and it may be an expiation of their sins"; God informed of Noah, "Seek forgiveness of your Lord — indeed He is ever Forgiving; He will send the sky upon you in abundance" (71:10–11), and of Hūd, "Then turn to Him in repentance; He will send the sky upon you in abundance" (11:52) — promising rain and fertility for particular peoples, as the close of our verse shows: "but they denied" — the messengers — "so We seized them for what they used to earn" [Qurṭubī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** heads his commentary with the principle of the verse — blessings come with faith, while unbelief brings torment — and reads it against 10:98, "Was there any town that believed, and its faith profited it, except the people of Jonah? When they believed, We removed from them the torment of disgrace in the life of this world" — a verse indicating that no city believed in its entirety except the city of Yūnus, for they believed only after the punishment had struck them [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** draws the contrast the section has been building: when God had mentioned that deniers of the messengers are tested with *ḍarrāʾ* as admonition and with *sarrāʾ* as drawing-on and plotting, He then mentioned that if the people of the towns had believed with their hearts a sincere faith, confirmed by deeds, and practiced the reverent fear of God outwardly and inwardly, leaving all that God has forbidden, He would have opened for them the blessings of heaven and earth — sending the sky upon them in abundance and bringing forth from the earth what they and their livestock live by, in the most fertile of living and the most abundant of provision, without weariness or toil. But they did not believe and did not fear; "so We seized them for what they used to earn," with punishments and afflictions and the removal of blessings and the multiplication of blights — and that is only part of the recompense of their deeds, for if He had taken them to account for all they earned, He would not have left upon the earth a single creature; as He said, "Corruption has appeared throughout the land and sea by [reason of] what the hands of the people have earned, so He may let them taste part of what they have done, that perhaps they will return" (30:41) [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** works out what "blessing" means: the Arabic *barakah* signifies increase or growth, and "blessings of the heaven and the earth" refers to all means of prosperity — proper and timely rain from the heavens, abundant and healthy produce of the earth, and, above all, carefree enjoyment of one's possessions with no anxiety to spoil the pleasure of them. *Barakah*, he observes, manifests in two ways: sometimes the thing itself increases in quantity, as in the reports of the Prophet's ﷺ blessing of a small pot from which a great number drank and were satisfied, or a whole army fed from a little food; and sometimes the quantity does not increase but its usefulness is multiplied manifold — a single morsel becomes a source of great strength while a large amount produces no result, an hour's work accomplishes what otherwise takes four or five. The verse, on his reading, states implicitly that *barakah* in heavenly and earthly things is achieved through faith in God and through *taqwā*, and that in their absence one is deprived [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** records the range of interpretation: the blessings from heaven and earth may refer to the opening of the heavens to send down rain so that crops grow abundantly on the earth (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī), but could also refer metaphorically and by extension to God's providing every manner of good, including security and peace (al-Rāzī), which can also be understood spiritually as peace for the soul [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the close reading and the variant: "Yet had the people of the towns believed and been fearful, We would indeed have opened upon them blessings from the heaven and the earth" — read *la-fataḥnā* or *la-fattahnā* — "but they denied, and so We seized them for what they used to earn" [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Barakāt* is the plural of *barakah*, a word of increase, growth and lasting good; **[Maʿārif]**'s two-part analysis of how increase happens — in quantity or in benefit — is the closest the commentaries come to defining it [Maʿārif]. *Fataḥnā ʿalayhim* — "We opened upon them" — is the Qurʾān's verb for the releasing of good, and it stands opposite the sealing (*ṭabʿ*) of 7:100: what God opens no one closes, and what He seals no effort opens [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Jalālayn]** preserves the recited variant *la-fattahnā*, with the doubled *tāʾ*, intensifying the opening [Jalālayn]. "*And been reverent*" (*ittaqaw*) **[Qurṭubī]** reads as *taqwā* from *shirk* in the first instance — the verse's primary reference is communities that denied their messengers; its principle extends to all that God has forbidden [Qurṭubī] [Saʿdī].

**Rulings.** Three rulings the commentators attach to the verse. First, the conditional "had they believed and been reverent" states a general law of God's dealing with nations, not a guarantee to individuals — the faithful may still live in straitness, and it may serve as expiation of sin [Qurṭubī]. Second, thanksgiving and *taqwā* are the conditions of *barakah* in one's provision; the two ways *barakah* manifests mean that a little with blessing can outperform much without it [Maʿārif]. Third, the verse answers the taunt embedded in the community's history: the people had asked why the believers were the straitened ones; the verse locates the straitness not in faith but in the denial of the towns [Saʿdī] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Cross-references.** "And if only the People of the Book had believed and been righteous, We would have expiated their misdeeds and admitted them to gardens of pleasure" (5:65); "And if they had kept up the Torah and the Gospel and that which was sent down to them from their Lord, they would surely have eaten from above them and from beneath their feet" (5:66); "And that you seek forgiveness of your Lord and turn to Him, He will provide you good provision for a specified term" (11:3); "And [mention] when We said, 'Enter this town and eat from it wherever you wish in abundance'" (cf. 2:58); "There was for Sheba a sign: two gardens, on the right and on the left — 'Eat of the provision of your Lord and be grateful to Him, a good land and a forgiving Lord'" (34:15); "And whoever fears God, He will make a way out for him and provide for him from where he does not expect" (65:2–3) [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The verse is the sūrah's economics in a single sentence. It does not say that denial brings no rain — the disbelieving world often has rain in abundance; it says that the opening of blessing, the *barakah* that makes rain into harvest and harvest into sufficiency and sufficiency into contentment, follows on faith and the fear of God, and withdraws with them [Maʿārif] [Saʿdī]. And it names the thing that forbids the opening: "but they denied" — the one word that has closed every story in this sūrah, from Noah's people to Shuʿayb's. What looks like a divine arrangement of agriculture is a divine arrangement of the heart: "Corruption has appeared on land and sea by what the hands of people have earned" — the land and sea, on **[Saʿdī]**'s reading, are simply the first to register what people have done with their souls [Saʿdī]. And the closing promise of 65:2 still stands for the reader who is not yet a ruined town: whoever fears God, God makes a way out.

## 7:97

*"Did the people of the towns feel secure from Our Might coming upon them by night, while they were sleeping?"*

**Meaning.** **[Qurṭubī]** gives the grammar first: the interrogative is one of denial and rebuke (*istifhām li-inkār*), the *fāʾ* a conjunction; the like of it is "Is it the judgment of the Age of Ignorance that they seek?" (5:50). "The people of the towns" — the reference is to Mecca and what is around it, because they denied Muhammad ﷺ; and it is said: the verse is general for all towns. *Baʾsunā* is Our punishment; *bayātan* is at night; "while they were sleeping" — while they lie down in rest and heedlessness [Qurṭubī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the question as an argument addressed to their own condition: "Did the people of the towns" — those that denied, by the context — "feel secure that Our might should come upon them by night while they are asleep, in their heedlessness, their delusion, their comfort?" And likewise the morning question: what could grant them security from that, when they have done its causes and committed of great crimes what would, for part of it alone, warrant destruction? [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** sets the three questions in their rhetorical function: the series emphasizes the foolishness of feeling secure in the life of this world, or secure from God's punishment, which may come in this world or in the next. It cites the Qurʾān's parallels — "Truly those who anticipate not the meeting with Us, and are content with the life of this world and feel secure therein, and are heedless of Our signs — it is they whose refuge shall be the Fire" (10:7–8); "Do those who have plotted evil deeds feel secure that God will not cause the earth to engulf them, or that the punishment will not come upon them whence they are not aware?" (16:45; cf. 12:107; 17:68–69; 67:16–17). The questions indicate that God's punishment may descend by night, while people are asleep and therefore unconscious and unaware, or in broad daylight while they are playing — distracted by their engagement with the affairs of this world, which is itself described in the Qurʾān as play and diversion (6:32; 29:64; 47:36; 57:20). And it records al-Rāzī's observation that God will send down punishment upon such disbelievers when they are in the greatest state of heedlessness [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** carries the line with its two questions intact: do the people of the towns feel secure from Our might coming upon them at night while they are sleeping, unaware of it? [Jalālayn]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gathers 7:96–99 under a single heading — blessings come with faith while unbelief brings torment — and then lets the four verses run as one indictment: the people of the towns who were sent messengers did not believe, so their punishment came by night or by day, and none but the losers felt secure from God's plan [Ibn Kathīr].

**Language.** *Bayātan* is the adverbial accusative of time from *bāta*, to spend the night: "by night," or "in the night-attack." **[Qurṭubī]** treats its partner *ḍuḥan* (7:98) as broad daylight, the hour of visibility and activity — so that the couplet covers the two halves of the human day, the darkness in which no one sees and the light in which everyone thinks himself seen and safe [Qurṭubī]. *Khāʾinīn* — no, *āminīn*, feeling secure — belongs in the sūrah to a family of words about safety: *amn* and *īmān* share the root, and the question the verse poses is whether one can have security without faith, certainty without submission [Qurṭubī].

**Cross-references.** "How many a town have We destroyed! Our might came to them by night or while they slept at midday, and their cry was only that they said, 'Indeed, we were wrongdoers'" (7:4–5) — the verse-pair that opens the sūrah and frames all its stories; "Then We seized them suddenly, while they were unaware" (7:95); "So when they forgot that by which they had been reminded, We opened to them the doors of every good thing until, when they rejoiced in that which they were given, We seized them suddenly" (6:44); "Or do you feel secure that He who is in heaven will not cause the earth to swallow you?" (67:16–17) [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The sūrah has now asked its question twice — once at the beginning, over the ruins of the towns it had not yet named (7:4), and now at the end of its prophet-stories, over the same ruins by name. The question is the same in both places: *did you feel secure?* The two times it names — sleeping at night and playing in the day — are between them the whole of an unconcerned life. And the answer the sūrah assumes is that nothing human borders the divine order so tightly that an unguarded hour cannot admit the end of the story. The people of the towns had believed themselves to be residents; the verse reminds them that they were guests who had not yet been asked to leave.

## 7:98

*"Or did the people of the towns feel secure from Our Might coming upon them in broad daylight, while they were playing?"*

**Meaning.** **[Qurṭubī]** sets out the readings and their senses. The two readers of the Ḥaram — Nāfiʿ and Ibn Kathīr — and Ibn ʿĀmir read the verse with the *wāw* unvocalized (sukūn), as the participle of conjunction, "or": *aw amina*, "or did they feel secure?" — the *aw* of granting a choice between the two questions, like "and do not obey among them any wicked one or ungrateful one" (76:24), or like "I struck Zayd or ʿAmr"; the meaning being: if you felt secure from one kind of punishment, you cannot feel secure from the other. And it may be the *aw* of one of two alternatives. The rest of the readers read it with the *wāw* vocalized and the interrogative *hamza* — *a-wa-amina* — making it the *wāw* of conjunction joined to the interrogative *hamza*, its parallel being "And is it that whenever they made a covenant..." (2:100) — that is, "and did they feel secure?" not "or." Then the phrase "in broad daylight while they were playing": the sense is while they are occupied with what does not profit them; al-Naḥḥās said: everyone who is engaged in what harms him and does not benefit him is called a player. He rounds out the entry with the lexicon of *laʿib* [Qurṭubī]. **[Saʿdī]** presses the question into an indictment: "Or did the people of the towns feel secure that Our might should come upon them in the morning while they play? What is it that could make them safe, when they have done its causes and committed of great crimes what would bring destruction?" [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** notes the same field: the daytime scene is not innocent recreation but the distraction of the heedless — "playing" because engagement with the affairs of this world is itself *laʿib* and *lahw* in the Qurʾān's vocabulary, "and the life of this world is nothing but play and diversion" (6:32) [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** carries the line: or do the people of the towns feel secure from Our might coming upon them in the daytime while they are playing? [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Ḍuḥan* is the forenoon, the bright broad day; *yalʿabūn* is from *al-laʿib*, play — **[Qurṭubī]** gives its morphology: *laʿiba*, *yalʿab*, the noun *al-laʿib* and *al-liʿb* alike; a man *tilʿābah*, one who plays much; a girl *laʿūb*; the participle's irony in the verse is that the Qruʾānic "play" is not the play of children, who know they are playing, but of adults who mistake their seriousness for reality [Qurṭubī].

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]**'s reading-facts yield a small ruling of worship: the variant *aw amina* / *a-wa-amina* is a reciting difference that changes the rhetorical movement but not the revelation — from "or" to "and" — and both are sound transmissions; he is content to record them side by side [Qurṭubī]. He also records al-Naḥḥās's definition, which expounds the verse rather than restricting it: whatever occupies a person to his own harm, with no benefit, is play [Qurṭubī].

**Cross-references.** "Then We seized them suddenly, while they were unaware" (7:95); "until, when they rejoiced in that which they were given, We seized them" (6:44); "Are they waiting except for the Hour to come upon them suddenly, while they do not perceive?" (43:66); "So who is more unjust than one who is reminded of the signs of his Lord and then turns away from them?" (cf. 18:57; 32:22) [Qurṭubī] [Saʿdī].

**Reflection.** The pair of verses, night and day, has an exactness to it worth pausing over. Night is when people cannot be vigilant; day is when they will not be — and the same might falls on both. The Qurʾān's chosen word for the day-half is "playing," which is to say: not only the hours of lust and violence are the danger, but the ordinary busy lightness of a life arranged around games it has never inspected. And the sūrah's next question (7:99) names the sum of the two: this is what it means to feel secure from God's plotting, and only a certain kind of people do it.

## 7:99

*"Did they feel secure from God's plotting? Then none feels secure from God's plotting except the people who are the losers."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** gives the sense: Did those who deny God and His messenger, and reject His signs, feel secure from God's drawing them on by degrees — *al-istidrāj* — through what He has blessed them with in their worldly life, soundness of body and ease of living, as He drew on those nations whose stories He has told before them? For none feels secure from God's plotting — that is, from its being a drawing-on while they remain in their unbelief and persist in their disobedience — "except the people who are the losers," and they are the destroyed [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** unfolds the same in the language of a warning that passes beyond the disbelievers to touch the faithful: *God's plotting* is His drawing them on from where they do not know and granting them respite; "and My plan is strong" (68:45; cf. 7:183); "and none feels secure from God's plotting except the losing people" — for whoever feels secure from God's punishment has not affirmed the recompense of deeds, nor believed in the messengers with the belief that is real. And this noble verse, he says, contains the most powerful warning that a servant should not feel safe about the faith he carries, but should remain fearful and anxious lest he be tried by a trial that snatches away what faith he has; he should always supplicate with the Prophet's ﷺ words — *O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion* — and should work and strive for every means that delivers him from evil when trials come; for the servant, whatever station he reaches, is never in assured safety [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** explains the term: God's plotting is mentioned in several verses and refers to God's ultimate control over the outcome of all events; it is frequently juxtaposed with the futility of human plotting by comparison (3:54; 10:21; 13:42; 14:46; 16:26; 27:50). Here, it may refer specifically to His seizing them with punishment while they were unaware (v. 95) [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** writes it short: did they feel secure from God's plotting — His drawing them on by degrees through graces, and then seizing them suddenly? None feels secure from God's plotting except the losing people [Jalālayn]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** closes the four verses together: the question, the taunt, and the verdict — those who do not believe in the face of God's signs are seized by night or by day, and only the losers are secure from the plan of God [Ibn Kathīr].

**Language.** *Al-makr* is planning that its object does not see. The Qurʾān applies it to God with the sense of *istidrāj*, drawing on by degrees: the giving that is not generosity but rope [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran]. **[Ṭabarī]** had already explained the word earlier in the sūrah, and he returns to the same explanation here: when attributed to God, *al-makr* is His disposing of affairs so that the heedless are taken unawares; the sense of guile is a human importation [Ṭabarī]. The verse's pairing is precise: *fa-lā yaʾmanu makra Allāh* — "so none feels secure" — the *fāʾ* making the second sentence the consequence of the first: the very act of feeling secure is the evidence of being lost, since it names someone who has misread his own condition [Qurṭubī].

**Belief.** The verse establishes the balance of fear and hope that the sūrah's teachers handle constantly: the servant does not despair of God's mercy — "who despairs of the mercy of his Lord except those who are astray?" (15:56) — and does not settle into security either, because God's giving may be a drawing-on and His respite an approaching of the day of reckoning. **[Saʿdī]**'s application is in a sense the verse's true audience: the one warned against feeling safe is not only the disbeliever of the towns but the believer reading the sūrah, who is to keep asking God to keep his heart firm and to take by every means available to him the road out of trial [Saʿdī]. The Prophet's ﷺ prayer he cites — *Yā muqallib al-qulūb, thabbit qalbī ʿalā dīnik* — is the faithful response to knowing that faith is not a possession but a gift that must be asked for again daily [Saʿdī].

**Cross-references.** "And they planned, and God planned, and God is the best of planners" (3:54); "And when We let the people taste mercy, they rejoice therein; but if an evil befalls them because of what their hands have done — then, behold, they despair" (10:21; cf. 10:21–22); "And how many a generation have We destroyed before them... And you would find no change in Our way" (cf. 35:43); "And I will give them respite — indeed, My plan is strong" (68:45); "And thus do We draw on those who deny the signs" (cf. 6:44); "Then sent down those who disbelieved... " (cf. 27:50) [Study Quran] [Ṭabarī].

**Reflection.** The sentence reverses the direction of ordinary fear. The people of the towns feared scarcity, defeat, humiliation — the ordinary bad news — and did not fear the good news, which is why the verse says that only the losers feel *secure*. What the damned hold onto as comfort — "we are flourishing, therefore we are fine" — is in the Qurʾān's grammar exactly the moment the trap is dampened. And **[Saʿdī]**'s turn of the knife is decisive: this fear is not for others. The believer looks at his own faith the way the sailor looks at weather, and asks God every morning to hold what he cannot hold. The verse is thus a duty of supplication in the shape of a warning.

## 7:100

*"Has it not become evident to those who inherit the earth after its people that, if We willed, We could smite them for their sins, and that We seal their hearts so that they do not hear?"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** gives the sense: Has it not become plain to those who are made successors in the earth after others before them were destroyed when they were its people — then they walked their way, did their deeds and grew insolent against the command of their Lord — that if We willed We would do with them as We did with those before them: seize them for their sins and hasten Our might upon them as We hastened it upon those whose land they inherited, destroying them for their sins; "and We seal their hearts" — We close them up — "so they do not hear" admonition or reminder with a hearing that profits? [Ṭabarī]. He records the early glosses on the opening verb: Mujāhid, *awalam yahdi* means "does it not make plain," and others said the same [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** quotes Ibn ʿAbbās to the same effect — "did We not make clear to them that had We willed We would have punished them because of their sins?" — and Mujāhid with him; then he transmits Ṭabarī's fuller explanation and adds the Qurʾān's own commentary: "Is it not guidance for them: how many generations We destroyed before them, in whose dwellings they walk? Indeed in that are signs, so will they not hear?" (32:26); and "Is it not guidance for them: how many generations We destroyed before them, in whose dwellings they walk? Indeed in that are signs, so will they not reason?" (20:128) [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same sense, verse by clause: *awalam yahdi*, "does it not make clear"; "those who inherit the earth" — the disbelievers of Mecca and those around them; "We smote them" — We seized them; "for their sins" — their unbelief and their denial; and on "We seal": the form *naṭbaʿu* is either a fresh sentence, "and We are sealing," or conjoined to *aṣabnā*, "We smite them and seal them," in which case the past stands in the place of the future [Qurṭubī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the verse as the sūrah's whole history lesson delivered in a single line: God says, alerting the nations who came after the destroyed nations — has it not become clear to those who inherited the earth after its people that, if We willed, We would smite them for their sins — for this is His *sunnah* with the first and the last? "And We seal their hearts, so they do not hear": when God warns them and they do not take heed, reminds them and they do not remember, guides them by signs and lessons and they are not guided, then God punishes them and stamps their hearts, so that rust and defilement cover them until they are sealed — no truth enters them, no good reaches them; they hear only what establishes the proof against them, as in His saying, "And We will turn their hearts and their eyes as they did not believe in it the first time, and We will leave them in their transgression, wandering blindly" (6:110) [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** frames the verse as the invitation that closes the section: after relating the events of the early people, these verses invite the people of Arabia, and the people of the world, to take lesson from them by abstaining from the deeds that incurred God's wrath and following the practices that led the prophets and their believers to success. The word *hādā* signifies to guide or to inform; the events have been made the subject of the verb "guide," so that they should serve as lesson and guidance for later generations who have inherited the land from its earlier owners — they too can incur God's punishment for their disbelief, as their ancestors met ruin for their disobedience. On "We seal their hearts": *ṭabʿ* is printing or stamping: these people took no lesson from the past, with the result that they incurred God's wrath, which sealed their hearts, so they are unable to listen to the truth. And he records the ḥadīth: *When a servant commits a sin for the first time, a black dot is placed on his heart; if he commits it a second and third time, second and third dots are placed; if he increases in sin without repenting, those black spots keep increasing until the whole heart is blackened* — which deprives a person of the natural faculty of distinguishing right from wrong, so that he receives evil as good and harmful as useful. This perversion the Qurʾān calls *rān*, the rust of the heart — "Nay, but what they used to do has become like rust upon their hearts" (83:14) — and this stage the verse names *ṭabʿ*. He adds a remark on the choice of "hear" rather than "understand": understanding is usually the result of listening to the truth, and all the faculties are controlled by the heart, so that when the heart is sealed the whole person is affected [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** notes the function: the stories of past peoples and their destruction for sin are meant as a warning for those who inherited the earth after them — all later peoples who might be similarly destroyed for their sins; in the context of the Prophet's life, this was perhaps directed specifically at the Quraysh (al-Rāzī), since, like the peoples of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot and Shuʿayb before them, they rejected their prophet and drove him from his native city. And it gathers the Qurʾān's teaching on the sealed heart: God may set a seal upon people's hearts so that they cannot hear or understand religious truth — a punishment particularly for wrongdoing or disbelief — found in many verses (2:7; 4:155; 6:46; 9:87, 93; 10:74; 16:108; 30:59; 40:35; 42:24; 45:23; 47:16; 63:3) [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the syntax: the interrogative *a-* with *walam* is for rebuke and is the subject of the clause; the readings and particles are as noted; and the last words are read "and We seal their hearts so they do not hear the admonition so as to reflect" [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Yahdī* is transitive here in a sense the lexicographers record: not "to guide" in the sense of granting faith, but "to make plain, to bring to one's knowledge" — Mujāhid's *yubayyin* [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif]. *Ṭabʿ* is the stamp; *khatm* the seal; the two words describe the same event from two sides: the impression made upon a thing and the barrier that closes it [Maʿārif] [Ṭabarī]. *Yarithūna al-arḍ* — "inherit the earth" — is the Qurʾān's word for succession among nations: the later people take up the fields and houses of the earlier, and the verse asks whether they understand what kind of title deed that is [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Maʿārif]** transmits the report of the black dots placed on the heart with each sin until the heart is blackened — the stage the Qurʾān names *ṭabʿ* — and joins it to 83:14's *rān* [Maʿārif]. **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s parallel chapter cites the sound ḥadīth of the believer's affair and the report on sudden death (discussed at 7:95) [Ibn Kathīr]. And the whole verse answers, by its own admission, to the reports of the earlier nations: the ruins the reader passes on the road are the *āthār* the verse appeals to [Study Quran].

**Rulings.** The verse is read by the commentators as a statement of the two kinds of divine action toward a heedless people: the outward act — smiting for sins, as with the towns whose land is inherited — and the inward act, which is worse, the sealing of the heart so that warning no longer reaches it [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī] [Maʿārif]. It also grounds the duty of taking admonition from history: the Qurʾān commands travel and reflection — "Travel in the earth and observe how was the end of those before you" (cf. 3:137; 6:11) — and the verse's question treats the ruins of the nations as a text God has set in the ground itself [Study Quran] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Cross-references.** "Is it not guidance for them: how many generations We destroyed before them, in whose dwellings they walk? Indeed in that are signs, so will they not hear?" (32:26); "so will they not reason?" (20:128); "And We will turn their hearts and their eyes as they did not believe in it the first time, and We will leave them in their transgression, wandering blindly" (6:110); "Nay, but what they used to do has become like rust upon their hearts" (83:14); "Travel in the earth and observe how was the end of those who denied" (3:137; cf. 6:11; 16:36); "And He is the One who has made you successors upon the earth and has raised some of you above others in degrees, that He may test you through what He has given you" (6:165; cf. 35:39) [Ibn Kathīr] [Maʿārif] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** With this verse the sūrah turns from the ruins to the reader. The six stories that began with Noah's ark and ended with Shuʿayb's town were not told for their own sake; they were placed in the ground of Arabia as evidence, and the question of 7:100 is whether the present possessors of the land will read the record that the land itself carries — that they inherit the earth after its people, and that the same condition that emptied those houses can flow into their own. [Maʿārif] notes that the prophet Mūsā's story is next, "after a few next verses" [Maʿārif]; the passage that closes here is the hinge between the prophets of the nations and the prophet of the Book. And the verse's final clause is the sharpest warning in the section because it describes a punishment that answers back to the crime: they did not listen, so they no longer can — until the loss is not merely of cities but of the organ with which God is heard. Every reader who has just passed through these ruined towns is invited to hear this final sentence while it can still be heard.
## 7:101

*"These are the towns — We relate to you some of their tidings. Their messengers came to them with clear proofs, but they were not to believe in what they had denied before. Thus does God seal the hearts of the disbelievers."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse as the sūrah's own summary of its prophet-stories: "These are the towns" — the peoples of Nūḥ, ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lūṭ and the people of Shuʿayb — "We relate to you some of their tidings," telling you of them and of what passed between them and the messengers of God sent to them, so that you may know that We aid Our messengers and those who believe, in the life of this world, against Our enemies and the people of unbelief, and so that those of your people who deny you may learn the end of those who denied God's messengers, and hold back from denying you, and turn to God's oneness and obedience [Ṭabarī]. He then takes up the difficult clause — "they were not to believe in what they had denied before" — and reports the difference of the early authorities: some said it means those destroyed peoples of the towns were never going to believe, when We sent to them, in what they had denied beforehand — namely on the day the covenant was taken from them when God brought them forth from the loin of Ādam; al-Suddī is among those who said so [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** records the same range in a tighter compass and adds a second reading: Mujāhid said the sense is that those disbelievers would not have believed even if, after their destruction, they were brought back to life — the like of "and if they were returned, they would return to what they were forbidden" (6:28); and Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Rabīʿ said it was in God's knowledge on the day He took the covenant from them that they would not believe the messengers. A third view he reports: they asked for miracles, and when they saw them they did not believe in what they had denied before seeing them — the like of "as they did not believe in it the first time" (6:110) [Qurṭubī]. On the seal: "Thus does God seal the hearts of the disbelievers" — the sealing that fell on the earlier peoples is the sealing that falls on the disbelievers of Muhammad ﷺ [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** sets the verse in the wider flow of the Qurʾān's argument: God has told the story of these towns and their destruction of those who disbelieved and their saving of the believers after they had been warned by clear proofs, and He cites as its frame "And We would never punish until We have sent a messenger" (17:15) and "That is some of the news of the towns which We relate unto you; of them, some are standing, and some have been reaped; and We wronged them not, but they wronged themselves" (11:100–101) [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** gives the purpose of the recounting: We relate to you of their tidings what brings a lesson to those who take lessons, a restraint upon the wrongdoers, and an admonition to the God-fearing; the messengers came to these deniers calling them to what holds their happiness, and God supported them with manifest miracles clearing the truth with a complete clearing — and it profited them nothing; so, because of their rejection and their turning away from the truth the first time, they were never guided to faith, being repaid for their rejection; as He said, "And We shall turn their hearts and their eyes as they did not believe in it the first time, and We shall leave them in their transgression, wandering blindly" (6:110); "thus does God seal the hearts of the disbelievers" [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** draws out the phrasing: *nabāʾ* in Arabic denotes great news, and the *min* of "some of their tidings" indicates that the events described are only some of a large number bearing the same lesson — a point worth carrying into the reading of every prophetic story in the Qurʾān. Their messengers came with clear signs and miracles, which are a definite means of deciding between right and wrong, yet they obstinately rejected the truth "only because they had once belied them." From this verse, he argues, we learn that miracles were given to all the prophets: the miracles of some are mentioned and most are not, and the silence of the Qurʾān about the latter does not allow the inference that they had none; as for the statement of Hūd's people, "you have not brought us a clear sign" (cf. 11:53), this verse shows that it was said out of obstinacy, or because they thought his miracles insignificant [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** reads it as a conclusion of the same kind as 11:100, where a nearly identical verse ends a similar series of narratives: the stories are recounted to the Prophet most specifically so that he would be assured that God aids His prophets and the believers against their enemies in this world (Ṭabarī) as well as rewarding them in the next, and so that he might warn his own people, the Quraysh, of the fate awaiting them if they persisted in disbelief (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabrisī). It records that for some commentators the "earlier denial" refers to the pretemporal covenant with all the Children of Adam (7:172), in which all human beings testified to God's Lordship — on their view, the earlier denial was manifested even then, in that they bore witness only reluctantly and insincerely (al-Rāzī and others) [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the plain sense: the messengers brought manifest miracles, but they would not believe when the signs came in what they had denied before the signs came; nay, they persisted in disbelief — thus God seals the hearts of the disbelievers [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Anbāʾ* is the plural of *nabāʾ*, news of weight — **[Maʿārif]** notes its sense of something great told [Maʿārif]. *Al-bayyināt* are the things that make a matter clear: proofs, arguments, miracles — **[Ṭabarī]** renders them *ḥujaj*, and **[Ibn Kathīr]** "evidences of the truth of what they brought" [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. The construction *fa-mā kānū li-yuʾminū* — "they were not such as to believe" — is, in Arabic grammar, a negated *lām* of denial stronger than a plain "they did not believe": it says not merely what happened but what could not have happened, given what they were [Qurṭubī] [Saʿdī]. And *ka-dhālika yaṭbaʿu* — "thus does God seal" — closes the circle with 7:100: the seal that is threatened in the previous verse is here declared to be God's standing treatment of those who deny [Qurṭubī].

**Cross-references.** "That is some of the news of the towns which We relate unto you; of them, some are standing, and some have been reaped. We did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves" (11:100–101); "And We would never punish until We have sent a messenger" (17:15); "And when your Lord brought forth from the Children of Adam, from their loins, their descendants, and made them testify of themselves: Am I not your Lord? They said, Yes, we have testified" (7:172); "And if you could see when they are made to stand before the Fire and say, 'If only we were sent back, and we would not deny the signs of our Lord'" (6:27–28); "And We shall turn their hearts and their eyes as they did not believe in it the first time" (6:110) [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The verse is the sūrah closing the file on the six towns and handing the reader its single conclusion: no one of them lacked a messenger, and no messenger lacked clear proofs. What they lacked was the willingness to believe what they had already been shown. **[Saʿdī]**'s paraphrase of the logic — because they rejected the truth the first time, they were not guided to it afterward — describes a law of the moral life still in force, and one the reader should dread more than any external punishment: the punishment of the sealed heart is that the evidence, which was clear yesterday, becomes invisible today. Compare the sūrah's earlier sentence about the same people: "And We shall turn their hearts and their eyes as they did not believe in it the first time" (6:110); the Qurʾān treats denial not as a single act but as a slope, and reading the stories of the towns is one of the means God has given to avoid the first step [Saʿdī] [Study Quran].

## 7:102

*"And We did not find most of them keeping any covenant; and indeed We found most of them to be defiantly disobedient."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** gives the sense: We found with most of the people of these towns that We destroyed, and whose tidings We recounted, no *ʿahd* — no fulfillment of what We charged them with, of the oneness of God, the following of His messengers, the working of His obedience and avoidance of His disobedience, and the abandonment of the worship of idols and carved stones. *Al-ʿahd* here, he explains, is the charge, the *waṣiyyah* — a meaning he had already established earlier in the commentary. "And We found most of them" — We found most of them to be nothing but disobedient to their Lord, abandoning His covenant and His charge [Ṭabarī]. He then records the early authorities: Mujāhid, on both halves of the verse, said it referred to the past generations; and in another report, on "a covenant," he specified the covenant God took from the Children of Adam in the loin of Ādam, which they did not keep; al-Rabīʿ transmitted from Abū al-ʿĀliyah from Ubayy b. Kaʿb the same — the covenant taken in the loin of Ādam; and Ibn ʿAbbās said: God destroyed the towns only because they did not preserve what He had charged them with [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** works through the grammar and the reports: the *min* in "We found for most of them no *ʿahd*" is superfluous in form but carries the sense of genus, and without it one might imagine the word meant a single covenant. Ibn ʿAbbās said: the covenant intended is the one taken from them at the time of the particles (*al-dharr*), and whoever breaks a covenant is spoken of as having no covenant at all — as though he had never been bound. Al-Ḥasan said: the covenant is the one charged to them with the prophets — to worship Him and associate nothing with Him. And it is reported from Abū ʿUbaydah: the disbelievers are of two sorts, and the majority are those with no trust and no fidelity, but among them are those who keep trust despite their unbelief, though these are few [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the two clauses together: "We did not find most of them keeping any covenant and indeed We found most of them defiantly disobedient" — that is, they broke the covenant of God confirmed to them, and We found them turning away from what they were commanded [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** sets out the two principal interpretations of the covenant: Ibn ʿAbbās and the Tafsīr Kabīr say it is the covenant of *alastu bi-rabbikum*, "Am I not your Lord?" — when all human spirits answered "Yes," and most people forgot that covenant on earth and turned to false gods; Ibn Masʿūd said it is the covenant of faith, as the Qurʾān says, "Except the one who has taken a covenant with al-Raḥmān" (19:87). And he draws out the practical sense: nearly every person, when he finds himself trapped in distress — however sinful — turns to God and promises in word or heart to be faithful to Him; the verse means that most people are untrue to such undertakings, and that faith itself is the great covenant from which most have slipped [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** explains the translation: "We did not find most of them [faithful to their] pact" renders idiomatically the Arabic that literally reads "We found no pact for most of them"; al-Rāzī notes that in being unfaithful to their pact with God, it is as if they had made no pact at all.
