"Most of them" may refer to the majorities among the peoples of Nūḥ, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lūṭ and Shuʿayb who rejected their messengers, or to most human beings in general; on the latter reading, the pact may be the pretemporal covenant of 7:172 requiring the acknowledgment of God's Lordship, and to be unfaithful to it means failing to continue recognizing His Lordship and His claim to obedience and worship during the course of earthly life [Study Quran]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** ties the verse to its predecessor's theme in one line: after the story of the towns, God says that He found not most of them true to their covenant, and that He found most of them rebellious — an account of the moral condition that made the seal of 7:101 necessary [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Jalālayn]** compresses it: We did not find most of them keeping any covenant, that is, any loyalty to the covenant from the day the pledge was made; nay, We found most of them indeed wicked [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Al-ʿahd* in Arabic is the binding charge and the promise to which one binds oneself — **[Ṭabarī]**'s *waṣiyyah*, the charge laid on a people; the verse's claim is not that they had made no promises but that the promises were not kept, so that in the moral ledger they may as well never have been made [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran]. *Fāsiqīn* is the plural of *fāsiq*, one who has gone out of bounds — from *fasaqa*, to leave, used of a date coming out of its skin; the defiant disobedient is one who has emerged from the shell of obedience [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. **[Qurṭubī]** also notes the unusual *min*: the grammarians call it surplus (*zāʾidah*), yet its function is real — it makes the negation total, denying not one covenant but any covenant of any kind [Qurṭubī].

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** draws from the verse the obligation of fidelity to covenants and undertakings, and, more importantly, the fact that faith is itself a covenant: the pledges people make to God in distress are debts they commonly forget when the distress passes, and the verse identifies that forgetfulness as the condition of the destroyed nations [Maʿārif]. **[Qurṭubī]**'s report from Abū ʿUbaydah warns against overreading: "most of them" (not all) leaves room for the trustworthy disbeliever whose fidelity is intact though his creed is not, and the exegetes were careful not to make the verse say more than it does [Qurṭubī].

**Cross-references.** "And when your Lord brought forth from the Children of Adam, from their loins, their descendants, and made them testify concerning themselves: Am I not your Lord? They said: Yes, we have testified — lest you say on the Day of Resurrection, 'Indeed, we were unaware of this'" (7:172; cf. 7:173); "Except the one who has taken a covenant with al-Raḥmān" (19:87); "And fulfill the covenant of God when you have taken it, and do not break oaths after their confirmation" (16:91); "Those who break the covenant of God after its ratification and sever what God commanded to be joined" (2:27; cf. 13:25); "And do not be like she who untwists her yarn after spinning it into strength" (16:92) [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The two halves of the verse are one diagnosis. A covenant is a thing kept; most of the towns had none kept, and so God names them not merely covenant-breakers but *fāsiqīn* — those who have left the enclosure. **[Maʿārif]**'s observation that every human being makes promises to God in moments of distress gives the verse a personal address: the reader recognizes the promises, and probably recognizes the forgetting. And if **[Study Quran]**'s reading is taken, the covenant in question is the oldest fact about a human being — the answer "Yes" to "Am I not your Lord?" spoken before the world — and the towns are destroyed for having spent their lives contradicting their own first word.

## 7:103

*"Then after them We sent Moses with Our signs to Pharaoh and his notables, but they wronged them. So look how the end of the corrupters was."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads it as the next link in the chain of messengers: after Nūḥ, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lūṭ and Shuʿayb — the prophets named from the beginning of the sūrah to this point — God sent Moses b. ʿImrān with His signs, meaning His proofs and evidences, to Pharaoh and his *malāʾ*, the assembled men of Pharaoh's people. "But they wronged them" — they disbelieved in the signs; and the *hāʾ* in "wronged them" refers back to the signs: they wronged Our signs by committing disbelief against them. He explains why *ẓulm* — normally "wronging" — can mean "disbelieving": wrong is the placing of a thing in other than its place, and disbelief in God's signs is precisely that, a placing of the signs in other than the place for which they were meant and a turning of them from the purpose for which they were intended [Ṭabarī]. Then the command: "So look how the end of the corrupters was" — look, O Muhammad, with the eye of your heart, at how the end of those who worked corruption in the earth came out — Pharaoh and his notables, when they wronged the signs God brought them by Moses; their end was that they were all drowned in the sea [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same reading with the same gloss of *ẓulm* — "the placing of a thing in other than its place" — and adds: "so look how the end of the corrupters was," that is, their final outcome [Qurṭubī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** sets the narrative in its place: after the messengers named — Nūḥ, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lūṭ, Shuʿayb — God sent Moses with His signs, the proofs and clear evidences, to Pharaoh, the ruler of Egypt in Moses's time, and to his chiefs, that is, Pharaoh's people; "but they wronged them," denying and disbelieving in the signs out of injustice and stubbornness on their part, and he cites the verse that diagnoses the crime exactly: "And they belied them wrongfully and arrogantly, though their own selves were convinced of them. So see what was the end of the corrupters" (27:14). Then he draws the picture of the end: those who hindered from the path of God and belied His messengers — look how God punished them: He caused them all to drown while Moses and his people watched; and the public drowning added disgrace to the punishment of Pharaoh and his people while adding comfort to the hearts of God's party, Moses and those who believed in him [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** reads v. 103 as the summary that the following verses will unfold in detail: then We sent after those messengers Moses, the one spoken to by God, the great leader and noble messenger, to a people of tyrants and giants — Pharaoh and his *malāʾ*, the nobles and the great among them — and He showed them of God's mighty signs what has no parallel witnessed. "But they wronged them": they did not submit to their due, and whoever does not submit to their due is a wrongdoer; rather they grew arrogant against them. "So look how the end of the corrupters was" — how God destroyed them and caused them to be followed by blame and curse in the world, and on the Day of Resurrection an evil reward [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** identifies the signs as either the verses of the Torah or the miracles of Moses, notes that Pharaoh was the title of the Egyptian kings and that the Pharaoh of Moses's time is said to be Merneptah; the phrase "they did injustice to them" means they showed indifference to the verses of God instead of gratitude and faith; and *ẓulm* here again signifies the use of a thing for a purpose opposite to that for which it was created; finally "so look how was the fate of the mischief-makers" is another invitation to take lesson from the events and think of one's own fate [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** provides the structural note: this verse begins a lengthy segment (vv. 103–56) concerning Moses, sent after the previous prophets of vv. 59–93, with other accounts of his life in 5:20–26, 10:75–93, 18:60–82, 20:9–97, 26:10–66, 27:7–14, 28:3–46, 40:23–45, 43:46–56 and 79:15–25; as with the earlier prophets, the opposition is led in part by the notables of the people to whom he was sent (cf. 7:60, 75, 88–90), who are guilty of having treated God's signs wrongfully (cf. 7:10) by disbelieving in them (Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī) or by turning others away from them (al-Zamakhsharī), as well as of being workers of corruption. The command "so behold" is addressed to the Prophet, indicating that these narratives were meant to give him confidence that God aids His messengers against their enemies [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** carries the line: then We sent after them — after the messengers mentioned — Moses with Our nine signs to Pharaoh and his council; but they mistreated them, disbelieving in them; so behold what was the end of those who work corruption by way of disbelief, when they were destroyed [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Al-malāʾ* is the assembly of the leaders and the elite who speak for the community — a word the sūrah has used for the notables of every people it has told (7:60, 75, 88–90, 109), and again here, so that Pharaoh's court stands in the same rank as the councils of the nations before [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran]. *Ẓulm* as wronging by displacement — putting a thing where it does not belong — is the commentators' shared definition, and it makes the verse's accusation exact: the signs were sent as guidance and were used as the occasion of denial [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif]. "So look" — *fa-nẓur* — is an imperative addressed to the Prophet, and the sūrah uses it as the pivot between story and lesson [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran].

**Cross-references.** "And they belied them wrongfully and arrogantly, though their own selves were convinced of them; so see what was the end of the corrupters" (27:14); "So We seized him and his hosts, and We cast them into the sea; then see how was the end of the wrongdoers" (cf. 28:40; 43:55); "And We caused the Children of Israel to pass through the sea, and Pharaoh and his hosts followed them in insolence and enmity, until, when drowning overtook him, he said, 'I believe'" (10:90); "Indeed, Pharaoh exalted himself in the land and made its people into factions" (28:4; cf. 28:3–6) [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Ṭabarī]** both record *ẓulm*'s definition as a technical point with a doctrinal edge: injustice is not merely taking what belongs to another, but putting any thing to a use for which it was not made — and on this definition, unbelief is injustice in the strictest sense, because it misuses the very faculty and the very sign by which God is known [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s use of 27:14 establishes that the denial of Pharaoh and his people was not honest doubt: they were "convinced" inwardly and denied outwardly, which is why the destruction is described as a public vindication of the truth rather than merely a punishment [Ibn Kathīr].

**Reflection.** With this verse the sūrah turns from the six ruined towns of the nations to Egypt — the longest of its stories, spanning more than fifty verses of Moses, Pharaoh, the sorcerers and the sea. The pattern introduced here governs all of it: signs are sent, they are "wronged," and the end of the corrupters becomes a public lesson. **[Study Quran]**'s reminder that the command "so behold" is addressed to the Prophet himself suggests what kind of book the Qurʾān's history is — not archival but pastoral, told to steady a man under pressure [Study Quran]. And **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s detail is the one the sūrah wants remembered: the drowning happened in view of those who had been enslaved, so that God's deliverance of the weak and His judgment on the tyrant were one event.

## 7:104

*"And Moses said, 'O Pharaoh, I am a messenger from the Lord of the worlds.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** gives the plain sense in a single line: God says that Moses said to Pharaoh, "O Pharaoh, I am a messenger from the Lord of the worlds" [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** draws out the address and its theology: this is the summary that the following verses will detail; "and Moses said" — when he came to Pharaoh calling him to faith — "O Pharaoh, I am a messenger from the Lord of the worlds," that is, a messenger from a great sender, the Lord of the worlds, the one encompassing the upper world and the lower, the one who rears all His creation with kinds of divine arrangement — among them that He does not leave them aimless, but sends to them messengers bearing good tidings and warnings; and He is the one whom no one is able to confront, claiming that He sent him when He did not [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** treats the verse as the foundation of the confrontation: it is a clear declaration by Moses before Pharaoh that he is a messenger of God, the Lord of all the worlds, and that his status of prophethood does not allow him to ascribe anything but truth to God, because the message given to the prophets is a sacred trust and tampering with it is a great sin. All the prophets are free of all sins and cannot do so; Moses tried to convince them that his truthfulness was beyond question, that he had never spoken a lie, and that his miracles were clear proof of his claim to prophethood [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** reads v. 104 with the verse that follows: Moses identifies himself to Pharaoh as a messenger of the Lord of the worlds; and one report has Pharaoh replying that he was lying, so that "obligated to speak naught about God save the truth" (7:105) is Moses's response to the charge (al-Thaʿlabī, al-Zamakhsharī). It notes that throughout the Qurʾān the prophets claim to bring a clear proof from God, and in this sūrah Ṣāliḥ (7:73) and Shuʿayb (7:85) make the same claim [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** renders it: Moses said, "O Pharaoh, I am a messenger from the Lord of the worlds" — to you, though he denied him [Jalālayn]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** introduces the verse as the opening of the debate that took place between Moses and Pharaoh, in which Moses refuted Pharaoh with the unequivocal proof and clear miracles in the presence of Pharaoh and his people, the Copts of Egypt: "And Moses said, 'O Pharaoh, verily I am a messenger from the Lord of all that exists'" — meaning the One who sent me is the Creator, Lord and King of all things [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]**'s comment on the same words stands in the middle of his discussion of the following verse's readings, where he identifies Moses as Moses b. ʿImrān [Qurṭubī].

**Language.** *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* is the title by which Moses names his sender — and **[Saʿdī]** observes the argument inside it: the *rabb* is the one who rears and completes by stages, and the *ʿālamīn* are all the worlds; a lord whose providence covers all creation is a lord entitled to send messengers to His creatures, and the Egyptian king who had been taken as a god over a patch of the Nile is not [Saʿdī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** renders the title's rule — "the Creator, Lord and King of all things" — so that the name itself contains the refutation of every claim of divinity beside God [Ibn Kathīr].

**Belief.** Two points of creed gather around the verse. The first is the integrity of prophecy: Moses's announcement of himself is inseparable from his statement that a messenger may speak nothing about God but the truth — the trust of the message [Maʿārif]. The second is the universality of God's lordship: the very form of the address to Pharaoh announces that the God of Moses is not a national deity contending with Egypt's gods, but the Lord of all the worlds, of which Egypt is a province [Saʿdī] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Cross-references.** "Go to Pharaoh — indeed, he has transgressed; and say, 'Would you like to purify yourself, and that I guide you to your Lord, so you should fear Him?'" (79:17–19); "And We sent Moses with Our signs and a clear authority to Pharaoh and his chiefs" (11:96–97); "Go, both of you, to Pharaoh — indeed, he has transgressed. And speak to him with gentle words that perhaps he may be reminded or fear" (20:43–44); "And say, 'We are the messengers of the Lord of the worlds'" (26:16) [Saʿdī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The first words of Moses to the most powerful man of his age are the least deferential sentence imaginable: not a petition but a definition. He does not say, "I have been sent to you"; he says, "I am a messenger from the Lord of the worlds" — placing Pharaoh, his court, his river and his kingdom inside a creation owned by someone else. Every call to God begins there, and **[Saʿdī]**'s point is that this is also the comfort of the caller: the one who sent him is the one who rears the worlds, and no tyrant is outside His care or His judgment.

## 7:105

*"[And] obligated to speak nothing of God but the truth. I have brought you a clear proof from your Lord; so send forth with me the Children of Israel."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** starts with the recitation difference, because it governs the meaning of the first clause. The majority of the reciters of Mecca, Medina, Basra and Kufa read *ḥaqīqun ʿalā an lā aqūla*, with the *ʿalā* written without the doubled *yāʾ*: "I am *ḥaqīq* that I say nothing about God but the truth" — the meaning of *ʿalā* here being that of the preposition *bāʾ*, as one says "I shot with the bow" and "upon the bow," "I came in a good state" and "with a good state." Some of those learned in the speech of the Arabs said that on this reading the sense is: eager, diligent that I not speak — or: it is fitting that I not say other than the truth. A group of the people of Medina read *ḥaqīqun ʿalayya an lā aqūla*: "it is binding upon me that I say nothing but the truth." Ṭabarī's verdict: both are well-known readings, close in meaning, recited by leaders of recitation, and whichever one recites, he has hit the right in his recitation [Ṭabarī]. Then the rest of the verse: "I have come to you with a clear proof from your Lord" — Moses said to Pharaoh and his notables: I have come to you with a demonstration from your Lord that testifies to the truth of what I say and the truthfulness of what I tell you, that God sent me to you as a messenger; "so send with me the Children of Israel" — let them go, O Pharaoh, with me. And Pharaoh answered: "If you have come with a sign, then bring it, if you are among the truthful" — a sign and mark witnessing to the truth of what you say [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** assembles the readings and the sense: *ḥaqīqun ʿalā* (the reading of Nāfiʿ) means "obligatory"; whoever reads *ʿalā* with the sense of *ʿalayya* renders it "obligatory upon me"; and in ʿAbdullāh's codex the word is *ḥaqīqun an lā aqūla* with *ʿalā* dropped; it is also said that *ʿalā* has the sense of *bāʾ* — "ḥaqīqun bi-an lā aqūla," as in the reading of Ubayy and al-Aʿmash, "bi-an lā aqūla," just as one says "I shot with the bow" and "upon the bow"; on this view *ḥaqīq* means *maḥqūq*, the passive: one who is made incumbent [Qurṭubī]. And on "so send with me the Children of Israel": the meaning is, release them — for Pharaoh used to employ them in harsh labor [Qurṭubī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the logic of the verse as an argument from Moses's own office: since such is God's affair, and He has chosen me and selected me for His message, it is incumbent on me that I not lie about Him and speak nothing about Him but the truth; for if I were to say other than that, He would hasten His punishment upon me and seize me with the grasp of a Mighty, Powerful one. This itself obliges Pharaoh and his people to submit to him and follow him — especially since he has come to them with a manifest proof from God of the soundness of the truth he brought — so that they were bound to act upon the purpose of his message; and his message has two great purposes: their faith in him and their following him, and the sending forth of the Children of Israel, the people whom God favored above the worlds, the children of the prophets and the line of Jacob, God's peace upon him [Saʿdī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** renders the clause and unpacks it: "proper it is for me that I say nothing concerning God but the truth" — it is incumbent and a duty for me to convey only the truth from Him, because of what I know of His might and power; "indeed I have come unto you from your Lord with a clear proof" — I brought unequivocal evidence that God gave me to prove that I am [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Maʿārif]** puts the emphasis where the verse itself puts it: in the light of these clear signs Pharaoh must believe him and let the Children of Israel go with him, free of his illegitimate surveillance [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** records the narrative situation: according to one account, after Moses articulated his claim to be a messenger from the Lord of the worlds, Pharaoh replied that he was lying, and in this verse Moses can be understood as responding to that charge by indicating that he would speak naught about God save the truth (al-Thaʿlabī, al-Zamakhsharī); it points to 7:169, where speaking truthfully about God is a condition of the Israelites' covenant. Clear proof (*bayyinah*) may refer to scriptural revelations, prophetic warnings, or evidentiary miracles (cf. 2:87; 3:105; 4:153; 5:32; 6:157; 7:101; 9:70; 10:13; 14:9; 16:44; 19:73); in this case it refers to the miracles Moses would subsequently perform (al-Ṭabrisī) as well as the force of his call to Pharaoh to accept the message and to send forth the Children of Israel (see also 20:47; 26:17; 44:18) [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** gives both the reading and the sense: "I am one for whom it is right to say nothing but the truth about God" — with the variant *ʿalayya*, "for me," in which case *ḥaqīqun* is the subject and "that I say nothing but the truth about God" its predicate; "truly I have come to you with a clear proof from your Lord; so send forth with me the Children of Israel," whom Pharaoh had enslaved [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Ḥaqīq* is the intensive adjective from *ḥaqq*: one who is true, or one made incumbent — **[Qurṭubī]** preserves the passive analysis *maḥqūq*, which turns the clause into "it has been made binding upon me," arguably the sharpest rendering, since it makes Moses's truthfulness not a personal boast but a constraint laid on him by his commission [Qurṭubī]. The *ʿalā* / *bi-* exchange (*ʿalā al-qaws* / *bi-al-qaws*) is the **grammarians'** standard example of prepositions used interchangeably in Arabic, and **[Ṭabarī]** uses it to show that the two readings, though different in wording, meet in meaning [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Bayyinah* is the clear proof that makes a matter plain — here, as **[Study Quran]** notes, the signs that follow [Study Quran].

**Readings.** *Ḥaqīqun ʿalā an lā aqūla* — the reading of the majority, including Nāfiʿ — against *ḥaqīqun ʿalayya an lā aqūla* (a group of the Madinans), and the codex of ʿAbdullāh b. Masʿūd's *ḥaqīqun an lā aqūla* with the preposition dropped, and the reading of Ubayy and al-Aʿmash *bi-an lā aqūla* [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī] [Jalālayn]. **[Ṭabarī]**'s ruling that both well-known readings are correct and that whichever one recites has attained the right is a model of his method on the recitation variants [Ṭabarī].

**Rulings.** The verse states the ethical constraint of the prophetic office — that a messenger speaks nothing about God but the truth — and ties it to Moses's fear of divine punishment should he deviate: "he would have seized me with the grasp of a Mighty, Powerful one" [Saʿdī] [Maʿārif]. On it, **[Maʿārif]** builds the doctrine that the prophets are protected from sin, since the trust of the message cannot survive tampering [Maʿārif]. And the political demand within the verse — "send forth with me the Children of Israel" — is the Qurʾānic charter of liberation from oppression: the struggle of Moses with Pharaoh begins not with a doctrinal debate but with the demand that an enslaved people be let go [Qurṭubī] [Saʿdī].

**Cross-references.** "Go to Pharaoh — indeed, he has transgressed — and say: would you like to purify yourself, and that I guide you to your Lord, so you should fear Him?" (79:17–19); "So go to him and say, 'Indeed, we are messengers of your Lord; so send forth with us the Children of Israel, and do not punish them; we have come to you with a sign from your Lord'" (20:47); "That you send forth with us the Children of Israel" (26:17); and "And speak to him with gentle words that perhaps he may be reminded or fear" (20:44) [Study Quran] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Reflection.** Three clauses, three duties. "I say nothing of God but the truth" is the messenger's integrity; "I have come with a clear proof" is the message's evidence; "send forth with me the Children of Israel" is the message's social demand. The sūrah later records what happened to the enslaved people, the plagues of Egypt and the sea; but the reader who wants the whole politics of the Qurʾān in a sentence can find it here: the same breath that declares God's lordship over Pharaoh demands the freedom of Pharaoh's slaves. And **[Saʿdī]**'s reason for Moses's honesty is the line to carry away — a messenger who lies about God is seized by God — which is why the truth of revelation is not a claim the prophet could afford to falsify, and not a claim the reader needs to doubt [Saʿdī].
## 7:106

*"[Pharaoh] said, 'If you have come with a sign, then bring it forth, if you are among the truthful.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the exchange in sequence: Moses had said, "I have come to you with a clear proof from your Lord — so send with me the Children of Israel"; and Pharaoh said to him, "If you have come with a sign" — that is, with a proof and a mark witnessing to the truth of what you say — "then bring it, if you are among the truthful" [Ṭabarī]. **[Maʿārif]** frames the demand by what precedes it: the Pharaoh, out of his obstinacy, did not listen to anything, but demanded: if you have come with a sign, bring it out, if you are among the truthful [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** notes that the sign Pharaoh asks for is understood variously: a proof of the truth of the message Moses brings (Ṭabarī) or a proof of God Himself (al-Ṭabrisī); and the two evidentiary miracles Moses produces in response — his thrown staff becoming a serpent and his hand turning white (see also 26:32–33) — were acts in which he had been previously instructed by God (20:18–22; 27:10–12; 28:30–32) [Study Quran]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** sets the verse inside the debate: God mentions a debate that took place between Moses and Pharaoh, and Moses's refuting of Pharaoh with the unequivocal proof and clear miracles, in the presence of Pharaoh and his people, the Copts of Egypt [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the challenge as the pivot on which the confrontation turns: Pharaoh answered the claim, the appeal, and the demand for the release of the Israelites with a request for evidence [Saʿdī]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the line: Pharaoh said to him, "If you have come with a sign as you claim, then produce it, if you are of those who speak the truth in this" [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Āyah* — here rendered "sign" — is in the Qurʾān's usage both the miracle and the verse: a thing that points beyond itself. **[Ṭabarī]**'s gloss, "a proof and a mark witnessing to the truth of what you say," preserves both senses, and his earlier gloss of *bayyinah* — the clear proof — shows the vocabulary of evidence running through the passage [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran]. *Ṣādiqīn* — "the truthful" — is the word Pharaoh chooses, and the sūrah will keep it in view: the demand is that Moses prove his truthfulness, and the meaning of the ensuing signs is precisely the vindication of a claim that Pharaoh already knew to be true [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Cross-references.** "And they belied them wrongfully and arrogantly, though their own selves were convinced of them" (27:14); "Then We sent Moses and his brother Aaron with Our signs and a clear authority, to Pharaoh and his chiefs, but they were arrogant and were a haughty people" (23:45–46); "Go, both of you, to Pharaoh — indeed, he has transgressed" (20:43); "And throw down your staff" (27:10; 28:31) [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The demand for a sign looks like a fair test and is not one. **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s use of 27:14 shows the sūrah's own diagnosis: the demand comes from a man "convinced" inwardly, so the sign is asked for not to settle a question but to postpone an answer. Still, God's practice in the Qurʾān is to answer even that demand: the signs are given, and the responsibility of those who asked for them becomes heavier, not lighter. The reader of the sūrah is put on notice about the difference between asking for evidence and asking for a way out.

## 7:107

*"So he cast down his staff, and behold — it was a manifest serpent."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** gives the plain sense: Moses cast down his staff, and behold, it was *thuʿbān* — a snake — "manifest," meaning apparent to whoever saw it, plainly a snake [Ṭabarī]. He then gathers the reports of the early authorities, and they are vivid. From Qatāda: it turned into a great snake — "and another said: like the city," so vast was it. And from Qatāda again: a snake that nearly leapt upon him. From al-Suddī: *al-thuʿbān* is the male of the snakes; it opened its mouth, put its lower jaw on the ground and its upper jaw against the wall of the palace, then headed toward Pharaoh to take him; when Pharaoh saw it he was terrified, jumped up and fouled himself, though that had never happened to him before, and screamed: "O Moses, take it, and I believe in you and will send the Children of Israel with you!" So Moses took it and it became a staff again. From Ibn ʿAbbās, by ʿAbd al-Karīm and Sufyān b. ʿUyaynah: he threw the staff and it became a snake which set one jaw below the dome and one jaw above the dome. From Ibn ʿAbbās by way of Saʿīd b. Jubayr: the staff became a huge snake with open mouth rushing at Pharaoh; when Pharaoh saw that it was heading for him, he leapt from his throne and begged Moses to restrain it from him, which he did. And from Ibn ʿAbbās, directly on the word: *thuʿbān mubīn* is the male snake. Then the report of Wahb b. Munabbih: when Moses entered upon Pharaoh, Pharaoh said to him, "Do I not know you? Did we not raise you among us as a child?" (cf. 26:18); Moses gave him back the answer he gave; and Pharaoh said, "Seize him!" — but Moses pre-empted him and threw his staff, and it was a manifest serpent; it charged at the people and they broke and fled, and twenty-five thousand of them died, some killing others; and Pharaoh fled, routed, until he entered the house [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the lexicon and the point of the adjective: *al-thuʿbān* is the huge male snake, the largest of snakes; "mubīn" means a snake in which there is no confusion, no ambiguity — the onlooker could not take it for anything else [Qurṭubī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** transmits the same reports in a compact form: ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭalḥah from Ibn ʿAbbās glossed *thuʿbān mubīn* as "the male snake," and al-Suddī and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said similarly; a report from Ibn ʿAbbās describes the snake opening its mouth and rushing toward Pharaoh, who jumped from his throne and cried to Moses for help to remove it from his way, which Moses did; and al-Suddī's comment is given in full — the snake opened its mouth and headed for Pharaoh to swallow him, placing its lower jaw on the ground with its upper jaw reaching the top of the palace wall; when Pharaoh saw it he was frightened, jumped and wet himself, as he had never done before, and cried, "O Moses! Take it away and I will believe in you and release the Children of Israel to you" — so Moses took it and it became a staff again [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Study Quran]** notes two things: the serpent was said to be of supernaturally large size and to have frightened Pharaoh from his throne (al-Thaʿlabī, al-Ṭabrisī); and, in a legendary account, the staff had been Adam's staff, bequeathed to successive prophets — including Shuʿayb, the father-in-law of Moses — until it reached Moses himself (al-Ṭabrisī); it is the staff God questions Moses about at the first encounter (20:17–18) and the staff Moses later uses to bring twelve streams from a rock (2:60; 7:160) and to part the sea (26:63) [Study Quran]. **[Maʿārif]** stresses the publicness of the act: *thuʿbān* signifies a huge serpent, and the adjective *mubīn*, "clear, prominent," is descriptive of the fact that this miraculous event took place manifestly before the eyes of Pharaoh's people, and was not performed secretly in a hidden or secluded place as the sorcerers usually do. He cites the tradition on Ibn ʿAbbās's authority that as the serpent moved toward Pharaoh, he jumped from his throne and sought shelter near the prophet Moses, and many of his courtiers died of extreme fear. On the possibility of the miracle, he adds: the transformation of a staff into a real serpent is not in fact totally impossible as it seems; it is surprising only because unusual; the miracle must be an unusual act, beyond the power of a common individual, and God shows miracles through His prophets so that people understand that they possess divine powers and are true prophets [Maʿārif]. **[Saʿdī]** gives the sense in its context: Moses cast his staff on the ground, and behold, it was a manifest serpent — a visible snake moving while they watched it [Saʿdī]. **[Jalālayn]** renders it: then he cast down his staff, and lo! it was a serpent manifest for all to see, an enormous snake [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Thuʿbān* is the great male serpent of the Arabic lexicon — the word for the largest of the species, whose open mouth the reports describe with two jaws spanning a palace [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Maʿārif]. *Mubīn*, from the root of *bayān*, means manifest, clarifying, unambiguous: **[Qurṭubī]** reads it as "a snake in which there is no ambiguity," and **[Maʿārif]** as the answer to the charge of sorcery, since sorcery is performed under cover with sleight and suggestion while this happened where all could see [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif]. *Alqā* — "he cast" — is the verb of throwing down, used of the sorcerers' rods later in the story, so that the sūrah's word for what Moses does and what the magicians do is the same, and the difference will be in the outcome [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn].

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** The reports gathered here are the sūrah's narrative furniture: al-Suddī's jaws of the serpent, its advance on Pharaoh, the throne abandoned and the cry for help and the promise to believe and release the Israelites — a promise that the sequel will show was extorted and broken [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. Ibn ʿAbbās adds the courtiers' deaths from terror; Wahb b. Munabbih's account has twenty-five thousand perishing in the panic as the crowd stampeded, and Pharaoh fleeing into the house [Ṭabarī]. And **[Study Quran]** records the tradition that the staff had passed down from Ādam through the prophets to Moses — a report it labels legendary, and which belongs to the narrative color with which the story was told rather than to the Qurʾānic text, which says only that it was Moses's staff [Study Quran].

**Rulings.** Two matters the commentators attach to this verse. First, the public character of the miracle is part of its proof: it was performed "before the eyes of Pharaoh's people," not in secret, which is what distinguishes it from sorcery — a distinction **[Maʿārif]** develops at length in connection with the next passage [Maʿārif]. Second, the miracle does not compel faith: Pharaoh saw the serpent with his own eyes, promised faith and the release of Israel, and later broke the promise (7:111–112), so that the verse stands as a warning that a sign witnessed is not the same as a heart changed [Ibn Kathīr].

**Cross-references.** "And throw down your staff — and when he saw it moving as if it were a serpent, he turned in flight" (27:10; cf. 28:31); "Throw down your staff. But when he saw it moving as if it were a serpent, he turned about, retreating" (20:19–21, at Moses's first encounter); "So Moses threw his staff, and behold, it swallowed up what they were falsifying" (7:117; cf. 26:45); "And it shall be a sign to you" (cf. 20:21); "We shall bring you a magic like it" (cf. 20:58) [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The sūrah tells this scene twice more from different angles — once in the meeting at the mountain (20:19–21), where the same staff in front of God's voice makes Moses himself flee, and once at the contest with the sorcerers (7:117), where it swallows their illusions. Its appearances are a kind of theology: the ordinary object of a shepherd's trade becomes God's instrument when God wills, and the difference between the staff on the ground and the serpent is never a difference in wood. The reports of Pharaoh's terror are almost comic — the god of Egypt leaping from his throne and soiling himself before a stick — and the sūrah will use that scene as one half of a deliberate pairing: the man who claims divinity must beg a prophet to save him from a reptile [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. Sorcery's whole economy is to make the false seem real; the sign reverses the exchange and makes the real appear.

## 7:108

*"And he drew forth his hand, and behold — it was white to the beholders."*

**Meaning.** **[Qurṭubī]** gives the lexicon and the reports. *Nazaʿa* means he took it out and revealed it; it is said the hand came from his bosom, or from his side under the arm, as in the verse "Insert your hand into your bosom; it will come out white without evil" (27:12) — "without evil," that is, without leprosy. Moses, he notes, was very dark-skinned (*asmar shadīd al-sumrah*); then he returned his hand to his bosom and it went back to its first color. Ibn ʿAbbās said: there was a shining light in his hand that illuminated what is between heaven and earth. And it is said: his hand would come out white like snow, gleaming; and when he returned it, it went back like the rest of his body [Qurṭubī]. **[Maʿārif]** develops the details: the verb *nazaʿa* signifies extracting something from another thing with force, and here indicates that Moses applied some force in drawing out his hand. The verse does not state the place he drew it from; in other verses we find two mentions — "enter your hand under your robe" (27:12) and "put your hand under your arm" (20:22) — indicating that he drew it out either from under his arm or from under his shirt. *Bayḍāʾ* means white; whiteness of a hand might be due to disease, which is perhaps why the Qurʾān added in other verses "without evil" (28:32; 27:12) to remove any possible doubt of a disease. And he records the tradition from Ibn ʿAbbās, reported by al-Qurṭubī, that the whiteness was not of the ordinary kind but had a light illuminating the surroundings; the word *al-nāẓirīn*, "the onlookers," indicates that the light was so surprising that people gathered to see it [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** describes the sign in its narrative place: Moses drew forth his hand and it was white to the onlookers, and reports from al-Thaʿlabī and al-Ṭabrisī that some commentators noted it was Pharaoh's request for a second sign that prompted Moses to put his hand into the hollow or sleeve of his cloak and then draw it forth "as white as snow" [Study Quran]. **[Ṭabarī]** has already read the verse with the one before it as a single unit — the staff and the hand, the two signs [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads them together as proof: "he drew out his hand" — from his pocket — "and behold, it was white to the beholders, without blemish"; these are two great signs indicating the soundness of what Moses came with and his truthfulness, and that he is the messenger of the Lord of the worlds [Saʿdī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** transmits it under the same heading with the sign of the staff, noting that the two are the evidentiary miracles with which Moses refuted Pharaoh [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Jalālayn]** renders: he drew forth his hand — he took it out from his bosom — and lo! it was white radiant for the beholders, and not its usual skin color [Jalālayn].

**Language.** The two verbs of the two signs are exact: *alqā* — throwing down, sending away from oneself — and *nazaʿa* — pulling out with force, the verb of extraction. The signs differ in kind as well: one gives away what he holds and it is transformed; one draws out his own body and it is transformed [Maʿārif]. *Bayḍāʾ* is the feminine of *abyaḍ*, white, and the Qurʾān elsewhere qualifies it "*min ghayri sūʾ*" — without disease — an addition the commentators explain as removing the suspicion of leprosy (*baraṣ*), which whitens the skin [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif]. *Lil-nāẓirīn* — "to the onlookers" — marks the sign's public character, as *mubīn* did for the serpent [Maʿārif].

**Rulings.** The verse gives the exegetes the occasion for a point of creed and a point of medicine at once: the miracle of the white hand was not vitiligo or leprosy — the Qurʾān's own "without evil" excludes the natural explanation — and its whiteness was luminous, radiating so that onlookers gathered to it [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif]. And the two signs together, staff and hand, are the two that Moses repeats at will: the sūrah will call them "nine signs" later (7:133 and 17:101), of which these are the first and most visible [Study Quran].

**Cross-references.** "Insert your hand into your bosom; it will come out white without evil, as another sign" (28:32); "Insert your hand into your bosom; it will come out white without evil — [part] of nine signs" (27:12); "And put your hand under your arm — it will come forth white without evil" (20:22); "And We had already given Moses nine clear signs" (17:101; cf. 27:12) [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The pairing of the signs is a kind of argument. The staff is what is outside a man — his tool, taken up and multiplied; the hand is what is closest — his own flesh, made luminous. Between them they cover the whole range of what a prophet might be thought to control, and neither belongs to him: the wood is a serpent by God's will and the skin is light by God's will. This is the boundary the sūrah is drawing throughout its Moses narrative, and the one Pharaoh cannot see: he thinks he is watching a magician's trick, when what he is watching is his own Creator rearranging, for a moment, the properties He gave to matter [Maʿārif] [Ibn Kathīr].

## 7:109

*"The notables among Pharaoh's people said, 'Indeed, this is a knowledgeable sorcerer.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the speakers and unpacks their accusation: the assembly of the men of Pharaoh's people, the nobles among them, said: "Indeed this" — meaning Moses — "is *sāḥir ʿalīm*," a knowing sorcerer: they meant that he takes people's eyes by his deception, until the staff is made to seem to them a serpent, and the dark-skinned man white, and things appear other than they are. He then performs a piece of linguistic archaeology worth pausing on: from this root is said *saḥara al-maṭar al-arḍ* — "the rain bewitched the earth" — when it strikes it well and cuts its plants from their roots and turns the earth over upon itself; the earth is then *masḥūrah*, bewitched. The sorcerer's sorcery, he says, was likened to that: he casts into the mind of the one he bewitches the impression of seeing a thing other than it is. And he cites Dhū al-Rummah's verse on the mirage: "and a sorceress of the eyes of the waterless wastes," the shimmering heat that makes a traveler see water where there is none [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** gives the scene and the psychology: those who do not believe would not believe even if every sign came to them, until they see the painful punishment; that is why the notables of Pharaoh's people, when the signs dazzled them and they did not believe, sought for them corrupt interpretations and said, "indeed this is a knowledgeable sorcerer" — that is, skilled in his sorcery [Saʿdī]. **[Qurṭubī]**, working through the passage's vocabulary, notes the same sense of *ʿalīm*: skilled in magic [Qurṭubī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** supplies the sequence: the chiefs and noblemen of Pharaoh's people agreed with Pharaoh's statement about Moses. After Pharaoh felt safe and returned to his throne, he said to the chiefs of his people, "This is indeed a well-versed sorcerer," and they agreed; they held counsel to decide what they should do about Moses; they conspired to extinguish the light that he brought and bring down his word; they plotted to portray Moses as a liar and a fake; they feared that he might lure people to his side by his magic, they claimed, and thus prevail over them and drive them away from their land — and what they feared occurred, as God said, "And We let Pharaoh and Hāmān and their hosts receive from them that which they feared" (28:6) [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Maʿārif]** explains why the word "knowing" was added: the Arabic *malāʾ* is used of influential chiefs; after seeing these miracles they said to the people that he was a great sorcerer — being ignorant of the divine powers of God they could say nothing else, having believed Pharaoh to be their god and having seen nothing in their lives but the magical charms of sorcerers. They added the word *ʿalīm*, "one who knows," showing their impression that the miraculous acts of Moses were of a kind that could not be performed by an ordinary magician; so they said he was a sorcerer of great knowledge [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** notes simply that the notables consider Moses a knowledgeable sorcerer — comparing 26:34, where it is Pharaoh himself who describes Moses to his notables as a knowledgeable sorcerer — and that they accuse him of seeking to expel Pharaoh from his land [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** renders the line and adds a note of narrative reconciliation: the council of Pharaoh's folk said, "Surely this man is a cunning sorcerer, outstanding in the art of magic"; and in Sūrat al-Shuʿarāʾ (26:34) these are actually Pharaoh's own words, so here it is as if they said it in consultation with him [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Sāḥir ʿalīm* — the pair defeats itself, and the commentators notice. *ʿAlīm* is the intensive of knowledge: the notables are conceding that what they saw exceeded the ordinary trade of the magicians, and then filing it anyway under the only category their worldview provides [Maʿārif] [Saʿdī]. **[Ṭabarī]**'s parallel between the sorcerer and the *sāḥirah* of the mirage is one of the commentary's sharpest philological moments: both make a beholder experience a thing as other than it is — the mirage as water, the staff as the limit of sorcery's power over a community's judgment [Ṭabarī].

**Belief.** The verse records the world's oldest explanation for a divine sign, and the commentators treat it as a doctrinal event, not merely a narrative one: ignorance of God's power forces the mind to reduce what it cannot deny to magic [Maʿārif]. **[Maʿārif]** then turns the verse into a lesson on the difference between miracle and sorcery — a discussion he develops under the following verses — with the observation that the sorcerers themselves, the men who knew the craft, would be the first to recognize that what Moses did was not theirs [Maʿārif] [Saʿdī].

**Cross-references.** "Pharaoh said, 'Indeed, this is a knowledgeable sorcerer'" (26:34, where the words are his); "They said, 'Indeed, these two are sorcerers who want to expel you from your land by their sorcery'" (20:63); "So their ropes and staffs seemed to him, from their sorcery, to be crawling" (20:66); "And they plotted, and We planned, while they perceived not" (cf. 27:50) [Study Quran] [Jalālayn].

**Reflection.** The notables' sentence is the world's shrug in the presence of God: rather than change their minds, they enlarge their category of magic. It would be easy to dismiss as ancient foolishness except that the diagnostic form recurs — any evidence that cannot be fitted to the reigning account of things is absorbed as an exception rather than allowed to redraw the picture. The sūrah will shortly set the magicians beside these men; the professionals watched the same events and fell down in prostration (7:120), which means the difference between the notables and the sorcerers was never the quality of the evidence [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr].

## 7:110

*"He desires to expel you from your land — so what do you command?"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the second half of the notables' speech as a bid for direction: they said, "He wants to expel you from your land" — Moses desires to drive you out of your land, O assembly of Copts, and to establish the Children of Israel before you in it — "so what do you command?" And he records the two ways the exegetes take the clause: it may be Pharaoh who said it, summoning his court to advise him; or it may be from the notables, addressing Pharaoh alone, "so what do you command?" — as tyrants and rulers are addressed: "What do you see concerning such-and-such?" [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** supplies the political mechanics: then they frightened the weak of understanding and the foolish of mind, warning them that Moses by this act of his "desires to expel you from your land" — that is, to evacuate you from your homelands — "so what do you command?" That is, they consulted among themselves about what they should do with Moses and by what means, as they supposed, the harm would be warded off from them; for what he had brought, if it were not met with what nullifies and refutes it, would enter into the minds of most people [Saʿdī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** continues the portrait of the conspiracy: they held counsel to decide what to do about Moses; they conspired to extinguish the light he brought and to bring down his word; they plotted to portray him as a liar and a fraud, fearing that he might lure the people to his side by his magic, as they claimed, and so prevail and drive them from their land — and what they feared came upon them (28:6) [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the clause's grammar and its two readings of speaker: "from your land" means from your kingdom with the Copts, by Moses putting the Children of Israel ahead of you; and "so what do you command?" is either Pharaoh's own question to his court or the notables' to Pharaoh alone, as the address to tyrants and rulers runs — "what do you think in such and such" [Qurṭubī]. **[Study Quran]** notes the accusation's content: the notables accuse Moses of seeking to expel Pharaoh from his land (cf. 26:34, where the same charge is in Pharaoh's own words) [Study Quran]. **[Maʿārif]** joins the verse to its predecessor: being ignorant of the divine powers of God, they could say nothing else but that he was a great sorcerer; and they framed the fear in the language of expulsion — the political charge that would let them rally the people behind the throne [Maʿārif]. **[Jalālayn]** carries the line: who would expel you from your land — so what do you command? [Jalālayn].

**Language.** The verb of the charge is *yukhrij* — to bring out, to expel, the same root used in Moses's demand "*fa-arsil maʿiya* Banī Isrāʾīl" and in the Exodus language of the sūrah: the notables turn the accusation around. The one who asked that a people be let go is charged with wanting to drive a people out — and the sūrah's audience, being told that the accusation is false, cannot fail to notice which people had actually been enslaved [Qurṭubī] [Saʿdī]. *Fa-mādhā taʾmurūn* — "what do you command?" — is addressed with the vocabulary of command even in counsel, and the **grammarians** note that this is how the great and the great ones are consulted in Arabic, the form of advice being phrased as a request for orders [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī].

**Rulings.** The commentators extract from the passage a lesson in the machinery of tyranny: the fear of losing a monopoly is converted into the language of national danger, and the notables then invite the ruler to decide, so that the responsibility for what follows is shared by "consultation" [Ẓāhir] — a reading spelled out by **[Ibn Kathīr]**, who traces the council to the point at which "they agreed on a plot," and by **[Saʿdī]**, who calls it consultation about how to repel a harm they had asserted rather than established [Ibn Kathīr] [Saʿdī]. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the grammatical note that the phrase may be Pharaoh's or the notables', and that either way the passage shows the court's speech in its three stages: admiration ("a knowing sorcerer"), alarm ("he wants to expel you"), and dependence ("what do you command?") [Qurṭubī].

**Cross-references.** "Pharaoh said, 'Indeed, this is a knowledgeable sorcerer who wants to expel you from your land by his sorcery; so what do you advise?'" (26:34–35); "They said, 'Indeed, these two are sorcerers who want to expel you from your land with their sorcery and do away with your exemplary way of life'" (20:63); "Then He will gather them and the hosts" (cf. 27:17); "And We let Pharaoh and Hāmān and their hosts receive from them that which they feared" (28:6) [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran] [Jalālayn].

**Reflection.** The charge "he wants to expel you from your land" is the oldest lie of embattled power: an enslaved people asking to leave is reframed as a plot to dispossess their masters. And the last clause — "what do you command?" — is the moment the sūrah shows how a court converts fear into policy, and how the machinery of the coming contest with the sorcerers is set in motion by a question rather than a decision [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī]. Read against what actually happens in the story, the accusation is exact in reverse: it is Pharaoh who will drive the Israelites out of Egypt in the end, in a single night, and it is the sea, not Moses, that will expel the notables from the land [Ibn Kathīr].
## 7:111

*"They said, 'Put him and his brother off for a while, and send gatherers into the cities.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the speakers and the first verb: the notables of Pharaoh's people said to Pharaoh, "Put him off (*arjiʾhu*)" — that is, delay him; and some of them said the sense is "imprison him." *Al-irjāʾ* in the speech of the Arabs is postponement: one says *arjaytu* and *arjaʾtu* this matter when one defers it; and from it is God's word "You may postpone (*turjī*) whom you will of them" (33:51). The *hamza* is the dialect of some of the tribes of Qays, who say *arjaʾtu*; the dropping of the *hamza* is the language of Tamīm and Asad, who say *arjaytu* [Ṭabarī]. He then sets out the recitations of the word: the generality of the reciters of Medina and some of the Iraqis read *arjihi* without the *hamza*, with *kasrah* on the *hāʾ*; some of the reciters of Kufa read *arjih* without the *hamza* and with the *hāʾ* unvowelled (*sukūn*), on the dialect of those who pause on the attached pronoun in connected speech when the letter before it is vowelled; the rest read *arjiʾhu* — the *hamza* — since the two are both established as dialects. He concludes by recording that both are languages of the Arabs and that both readings are sound [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** transmits Ibn ʿAbbās's comment in one line: "put him off" means delay him for a time; and "send to the cities" means to the districts and provinces of your kingdom, O Pharaoh, "gatherers" to collect magicians from the various lands. At this time magic was the trade of the day, widespread and popular; they had the idea that what Moses brought was a species of magic like the magic of their own sorcerers, and on that incorrect assumption they gathered every magician in the land to defeat the miracles he had shown. God said about Pharaoh, "Then verily, we can produce magic the like thereof; so appoint a meeting between us and you, which neither we nor you shall fail to keep, in an open place where both shall have a just and equal chance. [Moses] said: Your appointed meeting is the day of the festival, and let the people assemble when the sun has risen" (20:58–59) [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the counsel: then their opinion settled on saying to Pharaoh: "put him and his brother off" — that is, detain and give respite to the two of them — "and send into the cities people who will gather the people of the kingdom and bring every knowing sorcerer," to confront what Moses had brought; and he notes the fuller exchange preserved in Sūrat Ṭā Hā, where they proposed an appointed meeting which neither they nor Moses would fail, in a level place, and Pharaoh withdrew and gathered his plot and came (20:58–60) [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** gives the political frame: seeing the wonders of the staff turning into a serpent and the hand emitting strong white light, Pharaoh should have been convinced and believed, as logic and sense demanded; but it is the common practice of the wrongdoers that they always seek false interpretations of the truth in order to conceal it — so Pharaoh and his people belied him and told the people he was a great sorcerer who wanted to expel them from their homes and take over the country. The people of Pharaoh then suggested: "leave him and his brother alone for a while, and send men to the cities to collect and bring to you every expert sorcerer"; they said there were many expert sorcerers in their cities capable of defeating Moses, and soldiers should be sent to collect them; sorcery and magic being the custom of the day, the sorcerers enjoyed high status among the people [Maʿārif]. **[Ṭabarī]** also preserves Ibn ʿAbbās's fuller report of what lay behind the summons: after the decision, they asked, "With what does this sorcerer work?" They answered, "With snakes." They said, "By God, there is no people on earth more knowledgeable in the magic of snakes, ropes and staffs than we are; so what is our reward if we overcome him?" Pharaoh said, "You are my kin and my closest people, and I will do for you everything you wish." And in another report from Ibn ʿAbbās: Pharaoh said, "We shall not fight him except with one of his own kind" — so he prepared scholars from the Children of Israel and sent them to a town in Egypt called al-Faramā, where they taught them sorcery as boys are taught writing in the *kuttāb*, and they learned a great deal of magic; then Moses and Pharaoh set a time, and when the day came Pharaoh brought them and brought their teacher with them [Ṭabarī]. **[Study Quran]** sets the verse in the structure of the pericope, 7:111–26, which relates the competition between Moses and the sorcerers of Egypt — recounted too at 20:58–70 and 26:36–48 — and observes that the Bible provides a very short account in which it is Aaron who casts the staff (Exodus 7:8–13), while the Qurʾān's version becomes a meditation on the relationship between prophethood, miracles and sorcery, and on the differences between them in causality, power and reality. The notables' suggestion that Pharaoh put Moses and his brother off for a while is understood to be a suggestion that he detain them, either by delaying their departure or by imprisoning them (Ibn Kathīr, Ṭabarī) [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** renders the advice: put him and his brother off a while — postpone any decision about their affair — and send into the towns summoners, gatherers [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Arji* is the pivot of the verse and a small lexicon in itself: *al-irjāʾ* / *al-irjāʾ* is deferral, postponement [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. The participle *ḥāshirīn* — "gatherers," "musterers" — is from *ḥashara*, to collect a body of people; the same root will be used for the great gathering at the festival and, in the sūrah's background, for the gathering of humanity at the Resurrection, so that the mobilization of the sorcerers carries its own ironic resonance [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran]. *Al-madāʾin* — the cities, the provincial capitals — is the word for the administrative centers of the kingdom [Ibn Kathīr].

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** extracts from the passage a law of prophetic mission that he states explicitly: it is the usual practice of God that He sends His prophets with miracles appropriate to the demand of their time — in the age of Jesus, when Greek philosophy and medicine were at their height, the prophet was sent with the powers of restoring sight to the blind-born and healing the leper with a touch; in the age of the Arabs, boastful of oratory and eloquence, the Qurʾān was sent as the greatest miracle. In Moses's case the age's prestige science was sorcery, so the miracle was given in the sorcerers' own field, so that the people might observe their humiliation after they entered the contest [Maʿārif].

**Cross-references.** "So Pharaoh withdrew, devised his plot and then came back" (20:60); "And they said: We will not believe you until you produce for us a sorcery" (cf. 20:57, Moses accused of sorcery); "And the magicians came to Pharaoh and said, 'Indeed, there will be a reward for us if we are the victors'" (7:113; cf. 26:41); "Then, when the sorcerers came, Moses said to them, 'Throw down what you have to throw'" (10:79–80) [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The court's advice is a case study in the management of a miracle: because the sign cannot be denied, it is reclassified as the production of a superior expert, and the machinery of the state is put at the disposal of a contest in which the experts are meant to lose. **[Maʿārif]**'s sentence about the wrongdoers — that they always seek false interpretations of the truth in order to conceal it — is the verse's abiding lesson, and the summons into the cities is its consequence: rather than ask whether the sign was from God, the council asks where the best magicians may be found [Maʿārif]. The irony the sūrah plants here will flower at 7:120, when the experts whom the state has recruited to save its story turn out to be the first of its audience to see the truth.

## 7:112

*"To bring you every knowing sorcerer."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the clause as the purpose of the dispatch: "they will bring you every *sāḥir ʿalīm*" — every sorcerer knowing in his art — and he notes that the speech contains an ellipsis supplied by the obvious context: so Pharaoh sent into the cities gatherers, and they gathered the sorcerers [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s account of the summons shows the assumption that organized the whole enterprise: magic was the trade of the day and it was widespread and popular, and they had the idea that what Moses brought was a type of magic similar to the magic of their own time; because of this incorrect assumption they brought all the magicians in order to defeat the miracles he showed them, and he cites the challenge they sent to Moses: "Then verily, we can produce magic the like thereof; so appoint a meeting between us and you" (20:58) [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Study Quran]** adds the reports of what the marshalled sorcerers did: according to one report they boasted of their superior skill in the manipulation of staffs, ropes and snakes (Ṭabarī), and other reports indicate that the musterers sought out sorcerers capable of feats similar to those produced by Moses (Ibn Kathīr) [Study Quran]. **[Maʿārif]** notes the social fact behind the order: magic was the custom of the day, and from the Qurʾān's perspective the point of sending Moses with these particular miracles was that the people might clearly observe the sorcerers' predicament after they entered into a contest with him [Maʿārif]. **[Ṭabarī]**'s Ibn ʿAbbās report supplies the boast itself, quoted at 7:111: "By God, there is no people on earth more knowledgeable in the magic of snakes, ropes and staffs than we are" — the sorcerers' confidence, and the premise of their eventual collapse [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the clause as the intention of the council's plan: to gather the kingdom's people and bring every knowing sorcerer to set against what Moses had brought [Saʿdī]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the line and the variant reading: "to bring you every cunning sorcerer" — with a variant recitation *saḥḥār*, the intensive form, "master of sorcery" — to outdo Moses in the art of magic; and so they summoned them [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Saḥḥār* versus *sāḥir*: the reading preserved here belongs to the family of case-variants the commentators notice everywhere — *ʿalīm* with *saḥḥār* gives "every sorcerer, knowing"; the intensive *saḥḥār* sharpens the point, "every master sorcerer," and the exegetes treat the two as converging in meaning with the intensive carrying greater force [Qurṭubī] [Jalālayn]. *ʿAlīm* — knowing, expert — is the word the notables used of Moses at 7:109; the state now goes hunting for sorcerers with precisely the attribute they had used to explain him away [Ṭabarī] [Maʿārif].

**Cross-references.** "Then, when the sorcerers came, Moses said to them: Throw down what you have to throw" (10:79–80); "They said: O Moses, either you throw, or shall we be the first to throw?" (20:65); "And Pharaoh said: Bring me every knowing sorcerer" (10:79); "So gather your plot, then come in ranks" (20:64) [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The royal order is the theology of the passage in miniature. Moses says: the Lord of the worlds sent me. The court says: find the man with the best magic. Between those two sentences lies the whole distance the sūrah's Moses narrative is about to close, and the movement from the first to the second is not argument but appetite — the will to construe a sign as a skill. The verse also quietly establishes what will make the sorcerers' prostration at 7:120 so striking: they, of all people on earth, were the least likely to mistake God's act for their own craft, because they knew the craft exactly [Maʿārif] [Study Quran].

## 7:113

*"And the sorcerers came to Pharaoh; they said, 'Will there surely be a reward for us if we are the victors?'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** gives the plain reading and fills in the ellipsis: the mention of the sending is dropped because the sense is clear — Pharaoh sent into the cities gatherers, they gathered the sorcerers, and the sorcerers came to Pharaoh and said, "Indeed, there will be a reward for us" — a recompense for our overcoming Moses in your presence — "if we are the victors," O Pharaoh [Ṭabarī]. His Ibn ʿAbbās report supplies the fuller exchange: when they came to Pharaoh they asked, "What does this sorcerer work with?" They were told, "With snakes"; and they answered with their boast and their question about reward, and Pharaoh promised them kinship and everything they wished [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** puts the question in the mouth of men who do not move without a price: the sorcerers came to Pharaoh seeking from him the recompense if they should overcome, saying, "will there indeed be a reward for us if we are the victors?" [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** draws the contrast that the passage invites: the first thing the sorcerers did was to secure an assurance about the reward they would get in return for their performance, because worldly people are always after worldly gains — they do nothing unless sure of their gains — while the prophets and their disciples always declare, "I do not ask you any reward for it; my reward is only from the Lord of the worlds" (26:109). Moses asked for no wage to deliver his message; the professionals of Egypt asked for a wage before putting their spells on display [Maʿārif]. **[Qurṭubī]**, in the middle of the passage's discussion of the contest, gathers the reports on the sheer scale of the mobilization — material that belongs, strictly speaking, to the identity of these sorcerers: Ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam said they were twelve captains, with every captain twenty overseers, and under every overseer a thousand sorcerers, their chief being Shammūn according to Muqātil b. Sulaymān; Ibn Jurayj said they were nine hundred, from al-ʿArīsh, al-Fayyūm and Alexandria in thirds; Ibn Isḥāq said fifteen thousand sorcerers, and it is reported from Wahb likewise; and it was said twelve thousand; and Ibn al-Munkadir said eighty thousand; and it was said fourteen thousand; and it was said three hundred thousand sorcerers from the countryside, three hundred thousand from Upper Egypt, and three hundred thousand from al-Fayyūm and what adjoins it; and it was said seventy men, and it was said seventy-three — God knows best. And with them, it is reported, were ropes and staffs that three hundred camels carried, and the serpent swallowed all of it. Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Suddī said that when the serpent opened its mouth, its jaw became eighty cubits, its lower jaw set on the earth and its upper jaw [Qurṭubī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** frames the verse as the description of the conversation between Pharaoh and the magicians he had collected to defeat Moses: Pharaoh told them he would reward them and give them tremendous provisions, making them hope to acquire what they wished and to be among his private audience and best associates; and when they were assured of the accursed Pharaoh's promises, they went out to the contest [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Study Quran]** notes that the exchange is paralleled at 26:41–42, and draws out the ambiguity that the wording carries: on a literal level these verses articulate the worldly desire for reward on the part of the sorcerers and Pharaoh's worldly manner of fulfilling it, but the ambiguity of the language suggests that the sorcerers — and Pharaoh unwittingly but presciently — utter truths that will be realized in a manner other than the one they envision. The sorcerers are defeated by Moses, but their subsequent submission to God and to Moses can be understood as a spiritual "victory," for which they will surely have a reward with God; and it is noteworthy, though not mentioned in the well-known commentaries, that Pharaoh's promise that they shall be among those brought nigh (*muqarrabūn*) is identical to a description used elsewhere in the Qurʾān for prophets, angels and the most righteous who are rewarded with the highest Paradise (cf. 3:45; 4:172; 56:10–11, 88; 83:21, 28) [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** renders: and the sorcerers came to Pharaoh saying, "Will there indeed be a wage for us if we are the victors?" [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Ajr* is wage, recompense for work; *ghālibīn* is the participle of overcoming, and the two together make the sorcerers' terms of engagement explicit: this is a hire, and the goods are victory [Ṭabarī] [Maʿārif]. **[Maʿārif]** uses the word *muqābilah* — a return — and observes the professional logic: they negotiate before they perform. And **[Study Quran]**'s note that the negotiation is saturated with unmeant truth turns the vocabulary into the passage's irony: the wage they ask for will be paid, but not by Pharaoh, and the "nearness" promised them will be a nearness of another kind [Study Quran].

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** draws the moral distinction that the verse places before the reader: the sorcerers act for gain and the prophets act for God; the contrast between this verse and the prophets' refrain "I do not ask you for any reward" (26:109, 127, 145, 164, 180) is deliberate, and it teaches that the worth of a work is bound to its motive [Maʿārif]. **[Qurṭubī]**'s reports on the numbers of the sorcerers — from twelve captains to hundreds of thousands — illustrate how the *Isrāʾīliyyāt* expanded the scene, and the fact that he closes them with *Allāhu aʿlam* — God knows best — is his standard caution about such figures: no weight of interpretation rests on the count [Qurṭubī] *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)*.

**Cross-references.** "They said: Indeed, we shall then be the first to believe" (cf. 26:41–51, the fuller exchange of the sorcerers with Pharaoh, and their confession of faith); "I do not ask you for it any payment; my payment is only from the Lord of the worlds" (26:109, 127, 145, 164, 180 — the prophets' refrain); "Indeed, we hope that our Lord will forgive us our sins, since we are the first of the believers" (26:51) [Maʿārif] [Study Quran] [Jalālayn].

**Reflection.** The verse shows the birth of a conspiracy in the ordinary way — by negotiation — and the sūrah wants the reader to see what a hireling's religion looks like when the wage is on the table. **[Maʿārif]**'s contrast is the verse's whole force: prophets give without asking, professionals ask before giving, and the reader who has followed the sūrah's prophets from Noah to Shuʿayb has heard both kinds of voice. But the deepest irony the commentators find here belongs to **[Study Quran]**: the very words Pharaoh speaks — "you shall be among those brought near" — are the Qurʾān's words for the highest station of the righteous. He means a seat at court; the story will give him something else. The sorcerers will fall down in prostration before a God they had never served, having been brought near in a sense neither party intended when the bargain was struck [Study Quran].

## 7:114

*"He said, 'Yes, and indeed you shall be among those brought near.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse as Pharaoh's answer and his scale of rewards: he said, yes — there is a reward for you when you overcome Moses — and you shall be among the brought-near, meaning those close to me and my inner circle [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** unfolds the same: God describes the conversation between Pharaoh and the magicians he collected to defeat Moses; Pharaoh told them he would reward them and give them tremendous provisions, making them hope to acquire what they wished and to be among his private audience and best associates [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** draws out the ruler's technique: Pharaoh said, "Yes" — you have a reward — "and indeed you shall be among the brought near"; so he promised them both the reward and the drawing-near, and the elevation of rank with him, precisely so that they would exert themselves and expend their strength and capacity in overcoming Moses [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** adds the historical note: the sorcerers were invited from all over the country, and when they all came to Pharaoh they asked him for a reward if they should gain victory over Moses; he promised that in addition to their reward they would be included among those close to him; he cites the reports of their numbers — from nine hundred to three hundred thousand — and the quantity of staves and strings used, as great as could be loaded on three hundred camels (al-Qurṭubī) [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** develops the doctrinal resonance: Pharaoh replies that their reward will be to be among those brought nigh, meaning honored members of his court or his inner circle (Ibn Kathīr, Ṭabarī); and although these verses on a literal level articulate the worldly desire for reward and the worldly manner of fulfilling it, the ambiguity of the language suggests that Pharaoh — unwittingly but presciently — utters a truth that will be realized in a manner other than the one he envisions. The sorcerers are defeated by Moses, yet their subsequent submission to God and to Moses can be understood as a spiritual victory, for which they will surely have a reward with God. And it observes that Pharaoh's promise that they shall be among those brought nigh is identical to a description used elsewhere of prophets, angels and the most righteous rewarded with the highest Paradise (3:45; 4:172; 56:10–11, 88; 83:21, 28); the following verses suggest the sorcerers were martyred for their belief and may therefore be among those brought nigh in the Hereafter [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** gives it in ten words: he said, "Yes, and indeed you shall be of those brought near," with a variant recitation in the promise's phrasing noted in the verse before [Jalālayn]. **[Qurṭubī]**'s comment on the word *ʿalīm* in this passage — that Pharaoh's sorcerers were to be "knowing" in their art, experts summoned and paid precisely because expertise was required — stands behind the verse's economics: nearness to the throne is the price of a performance [Qurṭubī].

**Language.** *Muqarrabūn* — "those brought near" — is the passive participle of drawing near, and the Qurʾān uses it as a technical term of the highest station: the *muqarrabūn* are the nearest of the angels, and among human beings the foremost in faith (cf. 56:10–11, 88). **[Study Quran]**'s observation that the same word describes the blessed in the Hereafter makes Pharaoh's court language an accidental prophecy [Study Quran]. *Naʿam* — "yes," the answer given to a question of expectation — is the affirmative of grant, and the commentators hear the whole reward structure in its brevity: yes to the wage, plus the added nearness [Ibn Kathīr] [Jalālayn].

**Belief.** The verse yields a point about God's apportioning of rank: the sorcerers sought nearness to Pharaoh and gained it, and the sūrah will record that at the contest's end they were brought near to God instead. **[Study Quran]** reads the reversal as deliberate: the "victory" they asked a wage for became a spiritual victory they did not ask for, and their nearness came from the direction opposite to the one toward which they had been courting [Study Quran]. **[Maʿārif]** uses the same material for the doctrine of motive: worldly people do nothing unless assured of gain, and this verse is the master-professional's contract [Maʿārif].

**Cross-references.** "And those brought near [to God] will be far removed from it" (cf. 21:101); "Indeed, the righteous will be in bliss, and indeed, the wicked will be in Hellfire" (82:13–14); "And the foremost are the foremost — those are the ones brought near" (56:10–11); "And among them are those who have believed in him, and among them are those who have turned away" (cf. 4:55); "Thus We have made you a just community" (cf. 2:143) [Study Quran] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Reflection.** Two kings are offering nearness in this sūrah — Pharaoh to his sorcerers, and God to the righteous — and the contest at 7:116 will decide which kind of nearness the sorcerers actually get. **[Study Quran]** notes the implication with restraint: the men who accepted a place at the court of a false god were, by the end of the story, the first to prostrate to the true one, and the reader of the sūrah is quietly taught to re-read every worldly contract in light of who has the real authority to promote [Study Quran]. The verse also completes a pattern begun at 7:113: the negotiations between power and expertise are always about proximity — and the sūrah is about to show that proximity to power is not proximity to God.

## 7:115

*"They said, 'O Moses, either you cast, or we shall be the ones who cast.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the challenge and passes to Moses's reply, which the next verse opens: they said, "O Moses, either you cast — or we shall be the casters"; that is, either you throw down what you have, or we will throw down first [Ṭabarī]. **[Maʿārif]** puts the courtesies of the marketplace on display: on the day of the encounter the sorcerers asked Moses whether he would drop down his staff first or whether they should throw theirs first. Their question, he says, was perhaps to show their complacency about their art, though the form of their sentence indicated that they wanted to start first; but for giving an impression of power to their opponent, they put the question in that way — and since Moses had nothing to fear, he invited them: "you drop" [Maʿārif]. **[Qurṭubī]** draws the moral of their manners: they behaved with courtesy toward Moses — *taʾaddabū maʿa Mūsā* — and that very courteousness was the cause of their faith. He also gives the grammar of the construction *immā an tulqiya wa-immā an nakūna naḥnu al-mulqīn* — the *an* in the accusative position according to al-Kisāʾī and al-Farrāʾ, in the sense "either that you do the casting, or that we be the casters," with the poet's line "They said: the riding! — and we said: that is our habit" as its parallel. Then, on Moses's "Cast!", he reports al-Farrāʾ's observation that the speech has an ellipsis: Moses meant, "You will never overcome your Lord and never nullify His signs" — and this is among the marvels of the Qurʾān that cannot be matched in human speech, few words gathering abundant meanings.
