It is also said the command was a threat: begin the casting, and you will see the humiliation descending on you — for it is not permissible that Moses should have ordered them to sorcery; and it is said he ordered it precisely to expose their lie and their illusion [Qurṭubī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gives the wisdom of the order as the exegetes saw it: the magicians challenged Moses, "either you cast first, or we shall be the ones who cast" — and at 20:65 they say "or that we be the first to cast"; Moses said to them, "you cast first." It was said that the wisdom behind asking them to throw first is — God knows best — that the people might witness the magicians' sorcery first, so that when the spell had captured their eyes, the clear and unequivocal truth should come at the very moment they were waiting for it, making the truth even more impressive to their hearts. That is what happened [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Study Quran]** gathers the exegetical points: some commentators indicate that the sorcerers demonstrated proper etiquette by allowing Moses to choose who should cast first (al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī), even as they clearly preferred to cast first; some propose that it is precisely as a reward for their graciousness and proper demeanor toward Moses that God causes faith to enter their hearts, so that they ultimately come to believe in God and in Moses's prophethood (al-Rāzī); and this demonstration of *adab* toward Moses may have been preserved here because it suggests that the sorcerers possessed an inner sincerity that allowed them to perceive Moses as a person of noble character, deserving of respect. Moses responds graciously by letting them cast first, both because he understands their desire and because he is certain that God will aid him and that no sorcery can defeat a miracle, which has a divine cause and is inimitable through mere human agency (al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī); and their casting first allowed Moses to demonstrate more easily the powerlessness of their deceptions in the face of the truth (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī) [Study Quran]. **[Saʿdī]** reads their manner as defiance: when they came before Moses in the presence of a great crowd, they said — in the tone of one swearing an oath, and with indifference to what Moses had brought — "O Moses, either you cast what you have, or we shall be the casters" [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** also answers the objection this raises: sorcery is an impermissible act, and where it is used to oppose a prophet it becomes unbelief — so how could Moses grant them permission by saying "you cast"? He answers that it was certain in any case that the sorcerers would display their magic; the conversation was only about who should begin, and Moses allowed them to begin; another advantage of the strategy was that the people could see their performance, and the sorcerers would have all the time to display their art and turn their staves into snakes — after which the staff of Moses would become a serpent and devour all the snakes, so that the open defeat of magic should be exhibited before the people (Bayān al-Qurʾān) [Maʿārif]. **[Jalālayn]** renders: they said, "O Moses, either you cast your staff, or we shall be the casters — of what we have" [Jalālayn].

**Language.** *Alqā* — the verb of the whole passage — is to throw down, to cast away, and it will govern the next several verses: they throw, Moses throws, and finally the sorcerers themselves are "cast down" (*ulqiya*) in prostration at 7:120. The **grammarians** note the ellipsis in the exchange and the accusative *an* of the alternatives [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran]. **[Qurṭubī]**'s citation of "they said: the riding! and we said: that is our habit" is the grammarians' proof that the bare verbal noun can stand for a whole clause in Arabic — the Qurʾān's *immā an tulqiya* "either you to cast" is the same compression [Qurṭubī].

**Rulings.** Two rulings the commentators attach here. First, in **[Qurṭubī]**'s reading, the sorcerers' *adab* toward Moses was the cause of their faith: courtesy toward a prophet's person — even before belief — is not a neutral act, for God rewarded it with faith. Second, in **[Maʿārif]**'s reading, Moses's command "you cast" does not make him the sponsor of sorcery: the act was theirs in any case, the contest's order was a matter of demonstration, and the divine instruction that governs Moses's own act comes separately and expressly — "And We revealed to Moses: cast your staff" (7:117) [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif].

**Cross-references.** "They said: O Moses, either you throw, or we shall be the first to throw" (20:65); "Then, when the sorcerers came, Moses said to them: Throw down what you have to throw" (10:80); "And do not consume one another's property unjustly, nor offer it to the judges as a bribe" (cf. 2:188, on the prohibition of sorcery's use in judgment: cf. 2:102) [Ibn Kathīr] [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The etiquette of the challengers is a small hinge on which the sūrah's reading turns. They came as hired performers, sure of their craft; they arrive at the arena and offer their opponent the choice — and the exegetes saw enough in that courtesy to make it the seed of their conversion [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran]. Their confidence, meanwhile, is about to be answered not by a better spell but by the thing spells cannot do; and Moses's reply, "you cast," is the sound of a man whose trust is not in his staff but in the One who will command him when to use it [Maʿārif] [Ibn Kathīr].
## 7:116

*"He said, 'Cast!' And when they cast, they bewitched the eyes of the people and struck them with awe, and they brought forth a mighty sorcery."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** proceeds clause by clause: Moses said to the sorcerers, "Cast what you have to cast"; so the sorcerers cast what they had; and when they had cast it, "they bewitched the eyes of the people" — they made the people's eyes imagine, by the illusions and deceptions they produced, that the ropes and staffs were crawling; "and they struck them with awe" — they terrified the people by what they had bewitched in their eyes, so that they feared the sticks and ropes, supposing them to be snakes; "and they came with a mighty sorcery" — with a great illusion, a great deception [Ṭabarī]. He then gathers the early reports: from al-Suddī, that Moses said to them, "cast what you are going to cast," and they cast their ropes and staffs, and they were some thirty-seven thousand men, not one of them without a rope and a staff; and "they struck them with awe" means they put alarm (*farq*, dread) into them, so that Moses conceived fear in himself (cf. 20:67). From Ibn ʿAbbās, by ʿIkrima: they cast thick long ropes and long pieces of wood, and it was made to appear to him that they were crawling. From Ibn Isḥāq: fifteen thousand sorcerers drew up in ranks [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** supplies the technical account of the deception: "they bewitched the eyes of the people" means they caused them to imagine and turned their eyes from sound perception by the *tamwīh* that operates like sleight of hand and nimbleness of the fingers — the subject he had already explained in Sūrat al-Baqarah. Then he gives al-Farrāʾ's note on Moses's word "Cast": there is an ellipsis in the speech, and its meaning is that Moses said to them, "You will not overcome your Lord, and you will not nullify His signs" — which he calls one of the marvels of the Qurʾān that human speech cannot match, few words gathering many meanings. Or, he says, it was a threat: go ahead and cast, and you will see the disgrace that descends upon you — since it is not permissible that Moses ordered them to sorcery; and some said he ordered it in order to expose their lie and their dazzle [Qurṭubī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gives the purpose of the sequence: the wisdom behind Moses's instructing them to throw first — God knows best — is that the people might witness the magicians' sorcery first, so that when the magicians had cast their spell and captured the eyes, the clear and unequivocal truth might come at the moment they all anticipated it, making the truth even more impressive to their hearts. And he transmits Ibn ʿAbbās's description: the magicians threw thick ropes and long sticks, and they appeared to be crawling — an illusion they created with their magic; he sets beside it the Qurʾānic commentary, "So Moses conceived fear in himself. We said: Fear not! Surely you will have the upper hand. And throw that which is in your right hand! It will swallow up that which they have made. That which they have made is only a magician's trick, and the magician will never be successful, to whatever amount of skill he may attain" (20:67–69) [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the scene as a staged demonstration: Moses said, "Cast" — so that the people might see what they had and what Moses had — and when they cast their ropes and staffs, by their sorcery they seemed to be crawling snakes; so they bewitched the eyes of the people and struck them with terror, and brought a sorcery the like of which had no parallel [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** emphasizes what the verse does and does not assert: it indicates that this demonstration of their magic was just a bewitching of the eyes of the people, which made them see the staffs and strings as snakes leaping on the ground, while the strings and the staffs did not change physically — a kind of mesmerism, hypnotizing the minds of the onlookers. He then adds a caution: this does not mean that sorcery is confined to this kind, or that it cannot change something into another, since we have no proof against that; on the contrary, many forms and kinds of magic have been taken as reality, and the terms magic and sorcery are usually applied to all acts that seem to happen in an unusual way — sleight of hand, telepathic influence and mesmerism are sometimes called magic, and transformation of one thing into another through magic may be a possibility, though we have no valid reason of proof for or against it [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** collects the reports on the mechanics of the illusion: the sorcerers cast ropes and wooden staffs (26:44) that were painted so as to create the illusion of writhing snakes filling the ground, one upon another (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī); and others report that the sorcerers had placed mercury inside the staffs and ropes so that they began to move in the hot sun (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī), the competition being held in the open desert at the hottest time of day (see 20:58–59). It also notes that the sorcerers produced tricks that bewitched the eyes of the people through sleight of hand and illusion (al-Zamakhsharī), and cites 20:69, where their feat is described as merely a sorcerer's trick; and it recalls 20:67, where because of their display Moses conceived a fear in his soul [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** renders: he said, "Cast!" — a command permitting them to cast first as a means of manifesting the truth — and when they cast their ropes and staffs, they put a spell on the people's eyes, misleading them from perceiving the real state of those ropes and staffs, and overawed them, scaring them by making them appear to be slithering snakes, and produced a mighty sorcery [Jalālayn].

**Language.** The verse's three verbs are exact and cumulative: *saḥarū aʿyuna al-nās* — they bewitched the eyes, the seat of the deception being the perception of the audience, not the substance of the objects; *istarahbūhum* — they sought to put terror into them, the tenth form of *rahiba*, so that the crowd's fear became part of the trick; and *jāʾū bi-siḥrin ʿaẓīm* — they produced a great sorcery, the verdict of scale on what was, in **[Maʿārif]**'s terms, an operation on the eye and mind rather than on wood and rope [Ṭabarī] [Maʿārif]. **[Qurṭubī]**'s technical vocabulary — *tamwīh*, *shaʿwadhah*, nimbleness of the fingers — places the feat in the world of stagecraft and dexterity, which is precisely what the sorcerers themselves will admit when they see it undone [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Belief.** The passage establishes a distinction the exegetes treat as doctrinal: a miracle is an act whose cause is God's creative power, and sorcery is an act whose apparent wonder lies in the perception of the beholder or in hidden natural causation [Maʿārif]. **[Ibn Kathīr]**'s citation of 20:69 — "that which they have made is only a magician's trick" — is the Qurʾānic statement of the difference in kind, not degree: the two performances are not better and worse sorcery, but deception and truth [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** refuses to close the question of what magic can and cannot do, declining to assert that sorcery never transforms substances, since the texts do not settle it; what the verse settles is that *this* performance was illusion, because the Qurʾān says their eyes were bewitched and the staffs did not change [Maʿārif]. And **[Qurṭubī]**'s response to the objection that Moses authorized sorcery — that his "Cast!" was an exposure, or a threat, or a compressed speech whose full meaning was "you will not nullify God's signs" — preserves the prophet's integrity while letting the contest proceed on the sorcerers' own initiative [Qurṭubī] [Maʿārif].

**Cross-references.** "So Moses conceived fear in himself. We said: Fear not! Surely you will have the upper hand" (20:67–68); "That which they have made is only a magician's trick, and the magician will never be successful" (20:69); "And their ropes and staffs seemed to him, from their sorcery, to be crawling" (20:66); "Then, when they cast, they bewitched the eyes of the people and struck them with awe" (7:116, the same scene in the sūrah's own retelling); "God will soon bring it to naught; indeed God does not amend the work of corrupters" (10:81) [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The verse is the sūrah's study in how a crowd is captured: not by changing the world but by changing what the world appears to be. The sorcerers' feat is described entirely in terms of the audience — their eyes are bewitched, their awe is engineered — and this is why the miracle that follows is described entirely in terms of what happens to the objects: the staff becomes a serpent and eats. The day's contest is between a technique that works on people and an act that works on reality; and the sūrah trusts its reader to know which one has the Lord of the worlds behind it. **[Maʿārif]**'s caution about magic's possible realities keeps the discussion honest, but the verse's own diagnosis is not ambiguous: the people were made to see what was not there [Ṭabarī] [Maʿārif].

## 7:117

*"And We revealed to Moses, 'Cast your staff' — and behold, it devoured what they were falsifying."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse: God says that He revealed to Moses, "Cast your staff," and he cast it, and behold, it swallowed and devoured what they were bewitching falsely and vainly. Then the lexicon: one says *laqiftu al-shayʾ* — I swallowed the thing — *alqafuhu laqfan wa-laqafānan* [Ṭabarī]. He then gathers the reports of what happened. From Qatāda: Moses cast his staff and it turned into a snake and ate all their sorcery. From Ibn ʿAbbās, by ʿIkrima: Moses threw his staff and it was a snake swallowing what they falsified — it passed by nothing of their ropes and their sticks without devouring it; and then the sorcerers knew that this was a matter from heaven, and that it was not sorcery, and they fell prostrate and said, "We believe in the Lord of the worlds, Lord of Moses and Aaron." From al-Suddī the same, with God's words to Moses, "Fear not, and cast what is in your right hand; it will devour what they are falsifying"; and when they saw that, they prostrated and said, "We believe in the Lord of the worlds, Lord of Moses and Aaron." And from Ibn Isḥāq: God revealed to him to cast what was in his right hand; he threw the staff from his hand and it ranged over all they had thrown of their ropes and staffs — which were snakes in the eye of Pharaoh and the eyes of the people, crawling — and it began to devour them, swallowing them snake by snake, until nothing at all of what they had thrown could be seen in the valley, little or much; then Moses took it, and it was his staff in his hand as it had been; and the sorcerers fell prostrate and said, "We believe in the Lord of the worlds, Lord of Moses and Aaron — had this been sorcery, we would not have been overcome" [Ṭabarī]. **[Study Quran]** draws the doctrinal point from the delay: although Moses was expected to cast his staff to produce a great feat, he waits until God reveals to him the direct instruction to cast it — a command heard only by Moses (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabrisī) — indicating that the power and will behind this feat belong to God alone; it compares 3:49 and 5:110, where the miracles of Jesus are explicitly performed "by God's leave." Moses's staff becomes a serpent, as it did before Pharaoh alone in 7:107, and this serpent is reported to have literally devoured the sorcerers' deception — to have swallowed up the staffs and ropes that had been made to look like writhing serpents (Ṭabarī); and when Moses grasped the staff again, it returned to its previous form, as it did when God first instructed him in this miraculous sign in the holy valley of Ṭuwā (20:12) [Study Quran]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** heads the passage "Moses defeats the Magicians, Who believe in Him," and reads the verse as the moment at which God differentiated between truth and falsehood: He sent a revelation to His servant and messenger Moses, ordering him to throw the stick that he held in his right hand, and it swallowed straight away all the falsehood they showed — the magic that they caused the illusion with, making it appear real when it was not real at all. Ibn ʿAbbās said that Moses's stick swallowed all the ropes and sticks that the magicians threw; the magicians realized that this was from heaven and was by no means magic, and they fell in prostration. And he cites Muḥammad b. Isḥāq: it followed the ropes and sticks one after another until nothing that the sorcerers threw remained; Moses then held it in his hand and it became a stick again just as it was before; and the magicians fell in prostration and proclaimed, "We believe in the Lord of all that exists, the Lord of Moses and Aaron — had Moses been a magician, he would not have prevailed over us." And from al-Qāsim b. Abī Bazzah: God revealed to Moses to throw his stick; when he threw it, it became a huge manifest snake that opened its mouth and swallowed the magicians' ropes and sticks; on that the magicians fell in prostration; they did not raise their heads before seeing Paradise, the Fire, and the recompense of their inhabitants [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** gives the sequence in brief: "And We revealed to Moses: cast your staff" — so he cast it, and behold, it was a crawling snake that swallowed up all that they were falsifying, that is, all that they were denying and varnishing with illusions [Saʿdī]. **[Maʿārif]** treats the two-stage structure of the contest as instructive: Moses did not act until God's command came to him, and the command is phrased in the singular — "cast your staff" — so that the act is publicly Moses's and the power is God's [Maʿārif]. **[Jalālayn]** renders: and We revealed to Moses, saying, "Cast your staff," and lo! it swallowed up — read *talaqqafu*, with one of the original *tāʾ* letters omitted — the illusion they were creating, that which they were transforming by delusion [Jalālayn]. **[Qurṭubī]**, working through the verse's vocabulary, describes the serpent's devouring as literal: it consumed the ropes and sticks as the onlookers watched [Qurṭubī].

**Language.** *Talqafu* — it devours, gulps — from *laqifa*, to swallow, is read by the reciters with the elision of one of the doubled *tāʾ* letters in the original *tatalaqqafu*; **[Jalālayn]** notes the recitation's morphology, and **[Ṭabarī]** gives the dictionary forms *laqfan* and *laqafānan* [Ṭabarī] [Jalālayn]. "What they were falsifying" — *mā yaʾfikūn* — is from *afaka*, to turn a thing from its true state: their ropes were not snakes, and the verb itself says that their performance was a turning of appearances rather than a making of things [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī]. And the verse's structure — a revelation to Moses, a thrown staff, a devouring — makes the act a single sentence of three words in Arabic, so that the reader sees the economy of the miracle: no incantation and no apparatus, only a command and its execution [Ibn Kathīr] [Maʿārif].

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** The reports gathered here are the sūrah's fullest account of the end of the contest: the serpent traversing the valley, devouring the sorcery item by item until the ground was clear (Ibn Isḥāq); the staff returning to Moses's hand as a staff (Ibn Isḥāq, al-Suddī); the sorcerers' prostration and their confession that this was "from heaven, not sorcery" (Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Suddī); and the graphic tradition of al-Qāsim b. Abī Bazzah that they did not lift their heads before beholding Paradise and the Fire and the recompense of their inhabitants [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Study Quran]** labels as legendary the report that the staff had been Adam's and passed through the prophets to Moses (al-Ṭabrisī), and records that the serpent's size and effect are also reported in the tradition of al-Thaʿlabī [Study Quran].

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** draws from the verse the principle that a prophet's miracle is performed only under God's command and by His power, never by the prophet's intrinsic strength: Moses waits, then acts. He places it in the broader argument about miracles and sorcery — a miracle is beyond human power and directly a manifestation of Allah's power, which is why the Qurʾān ascribes it to God and not to the prophets, as in "but Allah threw" (8:17) [Maʿārif]. **[Study Quran]** makes the same point from the phrasing: the instruction is heard by Moses alone, which is how the reader knows the power behind the feat belongs to God [Study Quran].

**Cross-references.** "And throw that which is in your right hand! It will swallow up that which they have made" (20:69); "But when he saw it moving as if it were a serpent, he turned about, retreating" (20:21); "So Moses threw his staff, and behold, it swallowed up what they were falsifying" (7:117; cf. 26:45, "and it swallowed up what they were falsifying"); "And when Moses came at Our appointed time and his Lord spoke to him" (7:143); "So that God may confirm the truth by His words, even though the criminals dislike it" (cf. 10:82) [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Reflection.** The verse is the sūrah's clean separation of two things that looked alike: the sorcerers worked on eyes, and the staff worked on ropes. What the illusion had built out of paint, mercury and suggestion, the miracle simply ate — item by item, in public, until nothing remained in the valley. The professional sorcerers knew exactly what they were looking at, which is the whole reason they fell down: they had spent their lives producing effects like their own, and they had never seen an effect with no technique behind it [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran]. The lesson the sūrah draws from their fall is not that magic is weak but that God's act is not a stronger magic — it is a different order of thing altogether.

## 7:118

*"So the truth came to pass, and what they were doing was nullified."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** gives the sense: the truth appeared and became clear to those who witnessed and attended that gathering in the matter of Moses, and that he is God's messenger calling to the truth; and what they were doing of the falsehood of sorcery, its lie and its illusions, was nullified. He gathers the early glosses: Mujāhid, on "the truth came to pass," said: it appeared; and in another report on the full verse: the truth appeared, and the delusion they were working went away; and from Mujāhid again, "the truth came to pass" — Moses appeared [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads it as the assembly's public event: "so the truth came to pass" — that is, it was made clear and became manifest and was publicly established in that great gathering — "and what they were working was nullified," their sorcery dissolved [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** works out the verb and the scene: "the truth came to pass" means that it became plainly manifest and victorious over the sorcerers' deceptions (Ṭabarī) and that Moses's prophethood was validated (al-Ṭabrisī); the word *waqaʿa* also means to fall or alight, and some have suggested the sense here is that the truth alighted upon the hearts of the sorcerers (al-Zamakhsharī), since in the following verses they submit to God and to Moses. "Whatsoever they did" refers to the deceptions of the sorcerers, shown to be false. And it sets the verse among the Qurʾān's statements of the invincibility of truth against falsehood: "Say: Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished. Truly falsehood is ever vanishing" (17:81); "Nay, but We cast truth against falsehood, and it crushes it, and behold, it vanishes" (21:18); "God wipes out falsehood and verifies the truth through His Words" (42:24); and "That which you have produced is sorcery; God will soon bring it to naught... God verifies the truth through His Words, though the guilty be averse" (10:81–82) [Study Quran]. **[Jalālayn]** renders: thus did the truth come to pass — thus was it confirmed and made manifest — and that which they were doing in the way of sorcery was proved false [Jalālayn]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** includes the verse in his heading "Moses defeats the Magicians," reading it as part of the differentiation God made between truth and falsehood at that tremendous moment: truth was confirmed, and all that they did was made of no effect [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives Mujāhid's gloss — "the truth came to pass" means the truth appeared — and reads the two halves as a pair: the appearance of the truth is the disappearance of the illusion [Qurṭubī].

**Language.** *Waqaʿa* — to fall, to alight, to come to pass — is the verse's operative word, and **[Study Quran]**'s observation that it can mean "alight" opens the second reading the exegetes found: the truth did not merely win a contest but landed somewhere, and the following verses suggest where — in the hearts of the sorcerers, who at once prostrate themselves [Study Quran]. *Baṭala* — was nullified, came to nothing — is the verb of a thing becoming void, and it stands opposed to the truth's *wuqūʿ*: one arrives, the other vanishes [Ṭabarī] [Jalālayn]. *Mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — "what they were doing" — is in the imperfect, the tense of habitual action: not merely the spells of that afternoon but the work of their professional lives was shown to be nothing [Saʿdī] [Study Quran].

**Belief.** The verse states the sūrah's theology of truth in three words: truth arrives; falsehood's work is void. **[Study Quran]**'s gathering of the parallel verses shows how often the Qurʾān states this in the language of physical collision — truth is cast at falsehood and crushes it (21:18), falsehood vanishes (17:81), God wipes out falsehood and verifies the truth by His words (42:24). The commentator's note that the sorcerers' hearts may be the place where the truth alighted adds the interior dimension: the defeat of the magic was also the arrival of faith, and the verse may be describing both in one [Study Quran] [Qurṭubī].

**Cross-references.** "Say: Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished. Truly falsehood is ever vanishing" (17:81); "Nay, but We cast truth against falsehood, and it crushes it, and behold, it vanishes" (21:18); "God wipes out falsehood and verifies the truth through His Words" (42:24); "God will soon bring it to naught; indeed, God does not amend the work of corrupters" (10:81); "Rather, We hurl the truth against falsehood, and it breaks its head, and behold, it perishes" (cf. 21:18) [Study Quran] [Saʿdī].

**Reflection.** This is one of the shortest and heaviest verdicts in the sūrah: truth came to pass and their work was void. It matters that the sentence is written in the past tense of a single afternoon, and that the Qurʾān uses the same sentence for every confrontation of revelation with its opposition — it is the shape of the whole sūrah's history of the towns, condensed into an arena. **[Saʿdī]**'s phrase — the truth was publicly established in that great gathering — is precise: the point of the contest was not private conviction but public demonstration, and the crowd saw it. And **[Study Quran]**'s alternative reading of *waqaʿa* — that the truth alighted — turns the verdict into the beginning of the next verse rather than the end of this one: the truth fell, and it fell on the sorcerers [Study Quran].

## 7:119

*"So there they were overcome and turned back humiliated."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse as the outcome for the opposing side: Moses overcame Pharaoh and his hosts; "there" — *hunālik*, at that moment and in that place; "and they turned back *ṣāghirīn*" — they departed from that scene of theirs in humiliation, subdued, mastered. He gives the lexicon: *ṣaghira al-rajul yaṣgharu ṣagharan wa-ṣughran wa-ṣaghāran* — the man was small, low, humbled [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the grammar and the identification: *ṣāghirīn* is in the accusative as a state (*ḥāl*); the people of Pharaoh, and Pharaoh with them, turned away disgraced, subdued, defeated — "but as for the sorcerers, they believed." [Qurṭubī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the verse as the collapse of everything the challenge had promised: they were overcome in that station, and they turned back *ṣāghirīn* — contemptible, their falsehood dissolved, their sorcery vanished, and the purpose they had supposed they would achieve not obtained [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** reads the verb's subject as both parties — "they, that is, both Pharaoh and the sorcerers, were vanquished by Moses's feat" — and adds the narrative aftermath: Pharaoh reportedly released Moses from detainment or imprisonment (al-Ṭabrisī; see 7:111–12) [Study Quran]. **[Ibn Kathīr]**, having read the whole contest under one heading, has the humiliation as the counterpart of the vindication: the same moment that disgraced the court confirmed the messenger [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Jalālayn]** renders: thus were they — that is, Pharaoh and his people — there defeated, becoming humiliated; they ended up abased [Jalālayn]. **[Maʿārif]** carries the sequence: "so the truth prevailed and what they were doing became a nullity; so there they were overcome and turned humiliated; and the sorcerers were constrained to fall in prostration" [Maʿārif]. **[Ṭabarī]**'s reports from the previous verses already supply the specifics of the humiliation: the serpent ranging over the valley devouring their apparatus until nothing of it was seen, and (in a report from Wahb b. Munabbih concerning the earlier scene before Pharaoh) Pharaoh's own rout and flight [Ṭabarī].

**Language.** *Hunālik* — "there" — is a locative of distant reference that the Qurʾān often uses for the decisive moment, and the commentators read it as marking both the place of the contest and the turning point of the story [Ṭabarī] [Jalālayn]. *Inqalabū* — they turned back, they reversed course — is the verb of a movement reversed, and it comes to mean, as here, the reversal of fortune: they came to the field as the masters of the craft and left it as its refuted practitioners [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Ṣāghirīn* — humbled, brought low — is from the root of smallness, and **[Maʿārif]**'s rendering "turned humiliated" carries the sense of a condition imposed rather than chosen [Maʿārif] [Ṭabarī].

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]**'s note is the verse's doctrinal hinge, and he states it as a distinction within a single event: the humiliation fell on Pharaoh and his people, "but as for the sorcerers, they believed" — the same defeat that in one group produced shame produced faith in another, and by the next verse the sorcerers are prostrate [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran]. **[Study Quran]** adds the political detail that the release of Moses from detention followed the contest, so that the verse marks the failure of the very measure — "put him and his brother off" (7:111) — with which the court had begun [Study Quran].

**Cross-references.** "And they were overcome there and turned back humiliated" (7:119); "But they were defeated there, and they turned back humiliated — and the sorcerers fell down prostrate" (cf. 26:44–46); "So they were defeated there and became contemptible" (cf. 20:70 ff.); "And the wrongdoers will know to what end they shall return" (cf. 26:227); "So look how the end of the corrupters was" (7:103) [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The verse is a hinge in the sūrah's Moses cycle: the first confrontation ends, and the side that came to the field with every advantage — royal patronage, professional skill, popular awe and a promised reward — leaves it humbled, while the sorcerers who were meant to be its chief asset begin to change sides. **[Qurṭubī]**'s sentence "but as for the sorcerers, they believed" is the most economical statement of what has just happened, and it sets up the deepest irony of the narrative: the defeat that the court intended for Moses became the first harvest of his mission, and the men hired to defend Pharaoh's god were the first Egyptians to worship the God of Moses [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr].

## 7:120

*"And the sorcerers were cast down prostrate."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** records the verse's heading and, in the reports gathered through the previous verses, its content: Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Suddī and Ibn Isḥāq all narrate that when the sorcerers saw the staff devour their ropes and their wood, they knew that this was a matter from heaven and not sorcery, and they fell prostrate, saying, "We believe in the Lord of the worlds, Lord of Moses and Aaron" — and Ibn Isḥāq adds their own explanation of their conversion: "Had this been sorcery, we would not have been overcome" [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** makes the same point as a statement about expertise: the greatest of those to whom this mighty truth became clear were the people of that craft — the sorcerers — who know the kinds of magic and its particulars as no one else does; they recognized that this was a great sign of God's signs, one that no one has power over, and so the sorcerers were cast down prostrate [Saʿdī]. **[Study Quran]** works through the verse's diction and its theological payoff. "Were cast down prostrate" translates the same verb — *ulqiya* — used in the preceding verses for the casting down of the staffs, and this indicates the speed and force with which they prostrated themselves, as if they were literally "cast down," involuntarily, by what they had observed (al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī); the repeated verb also sharpens the contrast between their earlier "casting" of the staffs for the purposes of deception and their now "being cast" into sincere submission to God, and the commentators add that they made themselves prostrate in utter humility and submission (Ṭabarī). Then al-Rāzī's observation: the master sorcerers' immediate realization of the divine provenance of Moses's feat and their subsequent submission to God and Moses is an indication of the spiritual value of knowledge, for it was their advanced knowledge of their own art that allowed them to recognize that Moses's actions were not sorcery at all; full knowledge even of worldly sciences should engender an understanding of the limits and inadequacies of those sciences in relation to sciences based upon spiritual realities, particularly when one is directly confronted with the latter. For this reason, the Islamic tradition argues that each messenger was sent with miracles demonstrating an inimitable mastery of the leading arts and sciences of the people to whom he was sent: Moses is thus sent with miracles similar to, but utterly transcending, those of the sorcerers of his day, in a society where sorcery and the occult sciences were held in great esteem; Jesus was sent with healing miracles in an age when medical science enjoyed great prestige; and Muhammad was sent to the Arabs, whose major art form was poetry and eloquent speech, with a scripture whose literary and linguistic style was considered of unsurpassed beauty and power [Study Quran]. **[Maʿārif]** reads the falling as both their act and God's gift: the sorcerers were so overawed by the miracle of Moses that they fell in prostration, and this may also imply that Allah blessed them with favor and put them in prostration; they added "the Lord of Moses and Aaron" after "the Lord of the worlds" to make clear that the Lord worshipped by Moses and Aaron is, in fact, the Lord of the universe, and not Pharaoh as he pretended to be [Maʿārif]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** transmits the same sequence under his heading: the magicians realized that this was from heaven and was by no means magic, and they fell in prostration and proclaimed, "We believe in the Lord of all that exists, the Lord of Moses and Aaron — had Moses been a magician, he would not have prevailed over us"; and he preserves al-Qāsim b. Abī Bazzah's report that they did not raise their heads before beholding Paradise and the Fire and the recompense of their inhabitants [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]**, ending his commentary on the previous verse, has already announced the outcome: the people of Pharaoh and Pharaoh with them turned away humbled, "but as for the sorcerers, they believed" [Qurṭubī]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the line whole: and the sorcerers fell down in prostration [Jalālayn].

**Language.** The verse's verb is the passage's most deliberate repetition: the sorcerers came to the field to *cast* (*alqā*) their ropes; at the end of the day they are themselves *cast* (*ulqiya*) — the passive, with no agent named, which the commentators read as the moment's own force, prostrating them faster than any decision. **[Study Quran]**'s rendering, "were cast down prostrate," preserves the grammar's implication that the prostration was less a choice than an overwhelming [Study Quran]. And the word *sājidīn* — prostrate — is the posture of the created being before its Creator, so that the men who had just performed for Pharaoh's court end the scene performing for God, in the position of the angels [Maʿārif] [Study Quran].

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** preserves the report of al-Qāsim b. Abī Bazzah that the sorcerers did not lift their heads until they were shown Paradise, the Fire, and the recompense of the people of each — a report of the *āthār* rather than the Qurʾānic text, and one the commentators transmit as the tradition's way of describing the depth of the moment [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Ṭabarī]**'s accumulation of reports from Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Suddī and Ibn Isḥāq carries the conversion's own explanation — "had this been sorcery, we would not have been overcome" — words placed in the sorcerers' mouths by the tradition, and which state the verse's logic exactly: the professionals knew the ceiling of their craft, and what they had just seen was above it [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr].

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]**'s reading of the prostration as possibly God's favor — "Allah blessed them with favor and put them in prostration" — raises the theological point that faith itself is a gift granted, not only a conclusion reached, and that the moment of clarity was also a moment of grace [Maʿārif]. **[Qurṭubī]**'s use of the sorcerers' courtesy at 7:115 as "the cause of their faith" makes the same claim in narrative form: their treatment of Moses as a person deserving of respect bore fruit in their recognition of his Lord [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran].

**Cross-references.** "And the sorcerers fell down prostrate. They said: We believe in the Lord of the worlds, the Lord of Moses and Aaron" (7:120–122; cf. 26:46–48; 20:70); "And throw down what is in your right hand; it will swallow what they have made" (cf. 20:69); "So that God may distinguish the corrupt from the good" (cf. 3:179); "Say: Are those who know equal to those who do not know?" (39:9) [Ibn Kathīr] [Study Quran].

**Reflection.** The sūrah ends the first round of the Moses story with the best-placed witnesses in Egypt on their faces. The professionals of illusion had spent their lives producing effects that looked like the truth; the moment real power appeared in their field, they — of all people — needed no argument. **[Study Quran]**'s report of al-Rāzī's reflection is the passage's lasting lesson: their knowledge of their own art became the ladder by which they climbed to its limit and beyond it, and knowledge that does not do that has missed its purpose [Study Quran]. The verse's grammar says the rest: they intended to cast, and they were cast — and the hand that prostrated them was the same one that had sent the staff, whose owner alone is the Lord of the worlds, the Lord of Moses and Aaron [Maʿārif] [Jalālayn].
